Morse theory of loop spaces and Hecke algebras Thanks: KH supported by NSF Grant DMS-2003483. YT supported by NSFC Grant No. 11971256 and 12471064.
Ko Honda, Roman Krutowski, Yin Tian, Tianyu Yuan

Abstract
. Given a smooth closed n n -manifold M M and a κ \kappa -tuple of basepoints 𝒒 ⊂ M \bm{q}\subset M , we define a Morse-type A ∞ A_{\infty} -algebra C ​ M − ⁣ ∗ ​ ( Ω ⁡ ( M , 𝒒 ) ) CM_{-*}(\Omega(M,\bm{q})) , called the based multiloop A ∞ A_{\infty} -algebra , as a graded generalization of the braid skein algebra due to [ MS21 ] . For example, when M = T 2 M=T^{2} the braid skein algebra is the Type A double affine Hecke algebra (DAHA). The A ∞ A_{\infty} -operations couple Morse gradient trees on a based loop space with Chas-Sullivan type string operations [ CS99 ] . We show that, after a certain “base change”, C ​ M − ⁣ ∗ ​ ( Ω ⁡ ( M , 𝒒 ) ) CM_{-*}(\Omega(M,\bm{q})) is A ∞ A_{\infty} -equivalent to the wrapped higher-dimensional Heegaard Floer A ∞ A_{\infty} -algebra of κ \kappa disjoint cotangent fibers which was studied in [ HTY a ] . We also compute the based multiloop A ∞ A_{\infty} -algebra for M = S 2 M=S^{2} , which we can regard as a derived Hecke algebra of the 2 2 -sphere.

[bib,biblist]nametitledelim,

2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 53D40; Secondary 55P50, 57K31.

1.
Introduction

We continue the longstanding study of the relationship between the topology of a smooth manifold and the symplectic geometry of its cotangent bundle. Let
M
be a smooth closed
n
-manifold with
n\geq 2
and let
\bm{q}=(q_{1},\dots,q_{\kappa})
,
q_{i}\in M
for
i=1,\dots,\kappa
, be an ordered disjoint
\kappa
-tuple of basepoints. Motivated by the braid skein algebra of Morton-Samuelson
[
MS21
]
, we consider chains of the based loop space of the unordered configuration space
\mathrm{UConf}_{\kappa}(M,\bm{q})
, modulo certain skein relations.

The case of
\kappa=1
was studied by Abbondandolo-Schwarz
[
AS10
]
and Abouzaid
[
Abo12
]
, who showed that the wrapped Fukaya category of a cotangent fiber is
A_{\infty}
-equivalent to the differential graded algebra (dga) of chains of the based loop space.

When
\kappa\geq 1
, a natural generalization of the wrapped Fukaya category is the wrapped higher-dimensional Heegaard Floer homology (HDHF) of Colin, Honda and Tian
[
CHT24
]
. When
M
is a closed oriented surface of genus greater than
0
, Honda, Tian and Yuan
[
HTY a
]
showed that the wrapped HDHF
A_{\infty}
-algebra of
\kappa
disjoint cotangent fibers of
T^{*}M
is isomorphic to the Hecke algebra — also known as the braid skein algebra — of
\kappa
strands associated to
M
. (Analogous results also hold for punctured surfaces, but in this paper we assume
M
is closed for the sake of simplicity.)
When
M=S^{2}
, however, the above isomorphism does not hold. Indeed, the homology of the HDHF
A_{\infty}
-algebra is not supported in degree zero, whereas the braid skein algebra is supported in degree zero and is just an algebra.

This paper is comprised of the following three parts, with an eye towards understanding the
M=S^{2}
case:

(1) In Section
2
we define a Morse
A_{\infty}
-algebra
CM_{-*}(\Omega(M,\bm{q}))
, which we call the
based multiloop
A_{\infty}
-algebra of the manifold
M
and which can be viewed as a graded generalization of the braid skein algebra. The based multiloop
A_{\infty}
-algebra a priori depends on some auxiliary choices including a Riemannian metric on
M
, a Lagrangian action functional
\mathscr{A}_{V}
, and a pseudogradient vector field
X
of
\mathscr{A}_{V}
. Note that instead of the space of chains, we work with a smaller set of generators, i.e., the Morse chain complex. The generators are based loops of
\mathrm{UConf}_{\kappa}(M,\bm{q})
which are critical points of the Lagrangian action functional
\mathscr{A}_{V}
and the
A_{\infty}
-structure is defined by counting certain Morse gradient trees with respect to pseudogradient vector fields, coupled with a secondary type string operation
[
CS99
]
, as was introduced to Lagrangian Floer theory in unpublished work of Fukaya.

Corollary
1.4
below allows us to conclude that
CM_{-*}(\Omega(M,\bm{q}))
is an invariant of the smooth manifold
M
by appealing to Floer theory. This can be shown by solely working on the Morse-theoretic side by an argument similar to the one presented in
[
Maz21
]
, where the invariance of Morse homology (and the associated
A_{\infty}
-algebra) of a finite-dimensional manifold is established without invoking the isomorphism with singular homology. This corollary also suggests that the based multiloop homology is of its own interest from the perspective of string topology. As Corollary
1.5
and Theorem
1.7
show, the based multiloop homology constitutes a non-trivial (at least in these cases) deformation of a variant of the homology of
\kappa
-th product of the based loop space with itself. We anticipate that an interpretation of this deformation in terms of singular chains, as in e.g.,
[
Cha05
,
Iri18
,
Lau11
]
, on the
\kappa
-th power of the based loop space
\Omega(M,\bm{q})
may provide tools for further computations.

We additionally point out that the construction of the differential in the Morse model is akin to the Morse-theoretic construction of the secondary loop coproduct of Sullivan
[
Sul04
]
and Goresky-Hingston
[
GH09
,
HW23
]
, as explained in
[
CHO23
]
. Specifically, the specialization
\hbar^{2}=0
, which requires counting Morse flow lines with up to one switching, should be closely related to the loop coproduct. Furthermore, we anticipate that string topology operations and the whole TQFT structure
[
CG04
,
God07
,
Tam10
]
extend to the based (free) multiloop homology, though we do not pursue this direction here.

(2) In Section
3
we construct an
A_{\infty}
-morphism
\mathscr{F}
from
CW^{*}(\sqcup_{i=1}^{\kappa}T_{q_{i}}^{*}M)
, the wrapped HDHF chain group of
\kappa
disjoint cotangent fibers, to the based multiloop
A_{\infty}
-algebra
CM_{-*}(\Omega(M,\bm{q}))
, by counting rigid elements in a mixed moduli space which combines pseudoholomorphic curves and Morse gradient trees.

Remark 1.1

(Remark on ground rings)
.

The ground ring for the based multiloop
A_{\infty}
-algebra is
R=\mathbb{Z}
. The ground ring for the wrapped HDHF group
CW^{*}(\sqcup_{i=1}^{\kappa}T_{q_{i}}^{*}M)
and the map
\mathscr{F}
is
R=\mathbb{Z}
when
\kappa=1
or
n=2
, and
R=\mathbb{Q}
when
\kappa>1
and
n\geq 3
. The use of
R=\mathbb{Q}
is due to the use of Kuranishi replacements (see Section
3.3.2
), which require branched manifolds.

Remark 1.2

(Deformation parameter
\hbar
and base change)
.

There is a deformation parameter
\hbar
of degree
|\hbar|=2-n
that we informally refer to as the “Planck constant”. When
n>2
,
CM_{-*}(\Omega(M,\bm{q}))
and
CW^{*}(\sqcup_{i=1}^{\kappa}T_{q_{i}}^{*}M)
have the form
CM_{0,-*}(\Omega(M,\bm{q}))\otimes_{R}R[\hbar]
and
CW^{*}_{0}(\sqcup_{i=1}^{\kappa}T_{q_{i}}^{*}M)\otimes_{R}R[\hbar]
, and when
n=2
,
CM_{-*}(\Omega(M,\bm{q}))
and
CW^{*}(\sqcup_{i=1}^{\kappa}T_{q_{i}}^{*}M)
have the form
CM_{0,-*}(\Omega(M,\bm{q}))\otimes_{R}R\llbracket\hbar\rrbracket
and
CW^{*}_{0}(\sqcup_{i=1}^{\kappa}T_{q_{i}}^{*}M)\otimes_{R}R\llbracket\hbar\rrbracket
. The precise definition of
CM_{0,-*}(\Omega(M,\bm{q}))
is given in Section
2.3
and Definition
2.7
and that of
CW^{*}_{0}(\sqcup_{i=1}^{\kappa}T_{q_{i}}^{*}M)
is given in Section
3.1
. When
n>2
, we define
\hbar
-adic completions

(1.1)

\displaystyle CM_{-*}(\Omega(M,\bm{q}))\llbracket\hbar\rrbracket=CM_{0,-*}(\Omega(M,\bm{q}))\widehat{\otimes}_{R}R\llbracket\hbar\rrbracket\coloneqq\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}CM_{0,-*+(n-2)j}(\Omega(M,\bm{q}))\hbar^{j},

(1.2)

\displaystyle CW^{*}(\sqcup_{i=1}^{\kappa}T_{q_{i}}^{*}M)\llbracket\hbar\rrbracket=CW^{*}_{0}(\sqcup_{i=1}^{\kappa}T_{q_{i}}^{*}M)\widehat{\otimes}_{R}R\llbracket\hbar\rrbracket\coloneqq\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}CW^{*+(n-2)j}_{0}(\sqcup_{i=1}^{\kappa}T_{q_{i}}^{*}M)\hbar^{j},

The definition above is motivated by the fact that we are considering deformations of initial chain complexes (
A_{\infty}
-algebras) by a (possibly) non-zero graded formal variable
\hbar
; see also
[
Gan23
, Section 2.3]
.

We show that:

Theorem 1.3
.

\mathscr{F}
is an
A_{\infty}
-equivalence when
n=2
. When
n>2
it induces an
A_{\infty}
-equivalence of
\hbar
-adic completions.

The theorem has the following corollaries:

Corollary 1.4
.

The
A_{\infty}
-equivalence class of
CM_{-*}(\Omega(M,\bm{q}))
when
n=2
(resp.
CM_{-*}(\Omega(M,\bm{q}))\widehat{\otimes}_{R[\hbar]}\mathbb{Q}\llbracket\hbar\rrbracket
when
n>2
) is an invariant of the smooth manifold
M
and the positive integer
\kappa
.
In particular,
CM_{-*}(\Omega(M,\bm{q}))
when
n=2
(resp.
CM_{-*}(\Omega(M,\bm{q}))\widehat{\otimes}_{R[\hbar]}\mathbb{Q}\llbracket\hbar\rrbracket
when
n>2
) does not depend on the auxiliary choices used in the definition.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that the wrapped HDHF chain group is an invariant of the smooth manifold
M
and the positive integer
\kappa
.
∎

Corollary 1.5
.

When
M
is a closed surface
\not=S^{2}
,
CM_{-*}(\Omega(M,\bm{q}))
is quasi-equivalent to the braid skein algebra
\mathrm{BSk}_{\kappa}(M,\bm{q})\otimes_{R[\hbar]}R\llbracket\hbar\rrbracket
of Morton-Samuelson
[
MS21
]
.

Proof.

Combine Theorem
1.3
and the main result of
[
HTY a
]
.
∎

Remark 1.6
.

A curious observation is that, while the HOMFLY-PT relations were algebraically imposed as local relations for the braid skein algebra, in the case of the multiloop
A_{\infty}
-algebra
CM_{-*}(\Omega(M,\bm{q}))
, they are
not algebraically imposed
and
appear naturally
in the computation of
\mu^{2}
.

(3) In Section
4
we prove the following theorem:

Theorem 1.7
.

The based multiloop
A_{\infty}
-algebra
CM_{-*}(\Omega(S^{2},\bm{q}))
is quasi-equivalent to the dga
H_{\kappa}
, described below, for a particular choice of
{\bm{q}}
and auxiliary data.

Definition 1.8
.

Let
H_{\kappa}
be the unital dga over
\mathbb{Z}[\hbar]
, with generators
T_{1},\dots,T_{\kappa-1}
and
x_{1}
, and relations

(1.3)

\displaystyle T_{i}^{2}=1+\hbar T_{i},

\displaystyle\quad 1\leq i\leq\kappa-1,

(1.4)

\displaystyle T_{i}T_{i+1}T_{i}=T_{i+1}T_{i}T_{i+1},

\displaystyle\quad 1\leq i\leq\kappa-2,

(1.5)

\displaystyle T_{i}T_{j}=T_{j}T_{i},

\displaystyle\quad 1\leq i,j\leq\kappa-1,|i-j|>1,

(1.6)

\displaystyle x_{1}T_{j}=T_{j}x_{1},

\displaystyle\quad 2\leq j\leq\kappa-1,

(1.7)

\displaystyle T_{1}^{-1}x_{1}T_{1}^{-1}x_{1}+x_{1}T_{1}^{-1}x_{1}T_{1}=0.

The grading on the generators is given by
|T_{i}|=0
and
|x_{1}|=-1
.
The differential on the generators is given by
dT_{i}=0
and

(1.8)

\displaystyle dx_{1}=T_{1}T_{2}\cdots T_{\kappa-2}T_{\kappa-1}^{2}T_{\kappa-2}\cdots T_{2}T_{1}-1,

and extends to
H_{\kappa}
by the Leibniz rule.

Remark 1.9
.

The free loop space version of
CM_{-*}(\Omega(M,{\bm{q}}))
is the
free multiloop
A_{\infty}
-algebra
which is discussed in
[
KY23
]
.

Comparing Theorem
1.7
and Corollary
1.5
suggests that the DGA
H_{\kappa}
should admit a natural interpretation in terms of quantum topology. As it is of derived nature, it is necessary to develop a notion of a derived HOMFLYPT skein algebra of a surface. The natural guess here is that the approach of factorization homology developed in
[
BZBJ18
,
GJS23
]
should provide the necessary tool for such computations. Therefore we raise the following:

Question 1.10
.

What is the precise relation between the DGA
H_{\kappa}
and the appropriate notion of a derived HOMFLYPT skein algebra of
S^{2}
?

Finally, we are very grateful to Alberto Abbondandolo, who taught us the main ideas of the proof of Theorem
A.10
in Appendix
A
.

2.
The based multiloop
A_{\infty}
-algebra

As in the introduction,
M
is a smooth closed oriented
n
-manifold with
n\geq 2
and
{\bm{q}}
is an ordered disjoint
\kappa
-tuple of points on
M
.
Let
g
be a generic Riemannian metric on
M
and
|\cdot|
be the induced norm on
T^{*}M
.

2.1.
The Lagrangian action functional and pseudogradient vector fields

We follow the setup from
[
AS10
]
; also see
[
AS09
]
.

Consider the space of time-
1
continuous paths:

(2.1)

\Omega(M,q,q^{\prime})=\{\gamma\in C^{0}([0,1],M)\,|\,\gamma(0)=q,\gamma(1)=q^{\prime}\}.

We denote
\Omega(M,q)=\Omega(M,q,q)
and denote the subset of
\Omega(M,q,q^{\prime})
in the class
W^{1,2}
by
\Omega^{1,2}(M,q,q^{\prime})
.

Throughout the text we consider Lagrangians
L\colon[0,1]\times TM\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying the conditions given in
[
AS09
, Conditions (L1) and (L2)]
. For our purposes it suffices to restrict our attention to the Lagrangians associated with a potential, i.e., let
V\colon[0,1]\times M\to\mathbb{R}
be a function with small
W^{1,2}
-norm and let
L\colon[0,1]\times TM\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth Lagrangian function satisfying

(1)

There is a continuous function
\ell_{1}
on
M
such that for every
(t,x,v)\in[0,1]\times TM
with
x\in M
and
v\in T_{x}M
,

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{vv}L(t,x,v)\|

\displaystyle\leq\ell_{1}(x),

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{vx}L(t,x,v)\|

\displaystyle\leq\ell_{1}(x)(1+|v|),

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{xx}L(t,x,v)\|

\displaystyle\leq\ell_{1}(x)(1+|v|^{2}),

where
|v|=g(v,v)^{1/2}
.

(2)

There is a continuous positive function
\ell_{2}
on
M
such that
\nabla_{vv}L(t,x,v)\geq

\ell_{2}(x)I
, for every
(t,x,v)\in[0,1]\times TM
.

To any smooth (potential) function
V\in C^{\infty}([0,1]\times M)
we may associate Lagrangian

(2.2)

L_{V}(t,x,v)=\tfrac{1}{2}|v|^{2}-V(t,x),

satisfying (L1) and (L2). The metric
g
on
M
induces a metric
\tilde{g}
on
\Omega^{1,2}(M,q)
.

Given a Lagrangian
L\colon[0,1]\times TM\to\mathbb{R}
, we define the Lagrangian action functional

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{L}\colon\Omega^{1,2}(M,q,q^{\prime})\to\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{L}(\gamma)=\int_{0}^{1}L(t,\gamma(t),\dot{\gamma}(t))\,dt.

We write
\mathscr{A}=\mathscr{A}_{L}
when the Lagrangian
L
is understood from the context.
When
L=L_{V}
for some potential
V
as above, the action functional
\mathscr{A}
is twice Fréchet differentiable since for each
(t,x)
the map
v\mapsto L(t,x,v)
is a polynomial of degree at most
2
; see
[
AS10
, p.1583]
and
[
AS09
]
.

Remark 2.1
.

For a sufficiently generic Riemannian metric
g
on
M
,

(
\dagger
)

all the critical points
\gamma
of
\mathscr{A}_{L_{V}}
with
V=0
and
q=q^{\prime}
(i.e.,
g
-geodesics of
\gamma:[0,1]\to M
such that
g(0)=g(1)=q
) are nondegenerate and do not pass through
q
except at their endpoints.

The nondegeneracy/Morse condition is required in the definition of the differential
d
and the condition of not passing through
q
except at their endpoints ensures that
\Omega^{1,2}(M,q)
(together with the choice of
X\in\mathscr{G}
) behaves well when taking products and higher
A_{\infty}
-operations. (
\dagger
) can be realized by ordering the geodesics
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots
by nondecreasing action and inductively perturbing
g
on successively smaller neighborhoods
N(x_{i})
of
x_{i}\in\operatorname{Im}\gamma_{i}
.

A vector field
X
on a Hilbert manifold
\mathscr{M}
is a
(negative) pseudogradient
vector field of
f
if it satisfies the following:

(PG1)

f
is a Lyapunov function for
X
(this means a pseudogradient roughly points in the
negative
gradient direction of
f
);

(PG2)

X
is a Morse vector field whose singular points (= critical points of
f
) have finite Morse index;

(PG3)

the pair
(f,X)
satisfies the
Palais-Smale condition
;

(PG4)

X
is forward-complete, i.e., the flow
\Phi(t,x)
is defined for all
t>0
and
x\in\mathscr{M}
.

In addition to the above properties we usually require:

(PG5)

X
satisfies Morse-Smale property.

The following result summarizes properties of
\mathscr{A}
and, in particular, shows that there are plenty of (Morse-Smale) pseudogradient vector fields (see
[
AS10
, Appendix A.1]
and
[
AM06
]
):

Theorem 2.2
.

For smooth Lagrangian
L\colon[0,1]\times TM\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying (L1) and (L2) with all critical points of
\mathscr{A}_{L}
nondegenerate the following hold:

(i)

The action functional
\mathscr{A}_{L}
is bounded below.

(ii)

If all the critical points of
\mathscr{A}_{L}
are nondegenerate, then there exists a
smooth
vector field
X
satisfying (PG1)–(PG4). Moreover, for
L=L_{V}
the gradient
-\nabla_{\tilde{g}}\mathscr{A}
is a smooth pseudogradient.

(iii)

Any sufficiently small perturbation of
X
which coincides with
X
on a neighborhood of the critical points of
\mathscr{A}

is still a pseudogradient (i.e., satisfies (PG1)–(PG4)).

Let
\mathscr{K}
be the set of small perturbations of
X
as above which satisfy (iii) and hence are pseudogradients.

(iv)

A generic
\tilde{X}\in\mathscr{K}
satisfies the Morse-Smale condition (PG5).

Let
\,\mathscr{G}\subset\mathscr{K}
be the dense subset of Morse-Smale vector fields.

The generalization to
\kappa
-strand multipaths on
M
is straightforward:
Let
\bm{q}=(q_{1},\dots,q_{\kappa})
and
\bm{q}^{\prime}=(q^{\prime}_{1},\dots,q^{\prime}_{\kappa})
be (ordered, disjoint)
\kappa
-tuples of points on
M
and
{\sigma}\in S_{\kappa}
a permutation.
We define

\displaystyle\Omega_{\sigma}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})=\prod_{i=1}^{\kappa}\Omega(M,q_{i},q^{\prime}_{\sigma(i)}),\quad\Omega^{1,2}_{\sigma}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})=\prod_{i=1}^{\kappa}\Omega^{1,2}(M,q_{i},q^{\prime}_{\sigma(i)}),

\displaystyle\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})=\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{\sigma\in S_{\kappa}}\Omega^{1,2}_{\sigma}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\Omega^{1,2}_{\sigma}(M,\bm{q})=\Omega^{1,2}_{\sigma}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}),\quad\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q})=\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}).

Given the
\kappa
-strand multipath
\bm{\gamma}=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{\kappa})\in\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
, the Lagrangian action functional
\mathscr{A}_{L}\colon\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})\to\mathbb{R}
,
also denoted
\mathscr{A}_{V}
or
\mathscr{A}_{L_{V}}
, is given by:

(2.3)

\mathscr{A}_{L}(\bm{\gamma})=\sum_{i}\mathscr{A}_{L}(\gamma_{i}).

If
\bm{\gamma}\in\Omega^{1,2}_{\sigma}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
, then
\sigma
is called the
permutation type of
\bm{\gamma}
.

2.2.
The switching map

In this section, we introduce
the switching map
which will play a crucial role in the definition of the
A_{\infty}
-operations of the based multiloop
A_{\infty}
-algebra. Let
\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
for some integer
m\geq 1
be the subset of
\Omega(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
of elements of class
W^{m,2}
and let
\Delta_{M}
be the diagonal in
M\times M
. For
1\leq i<j\leq\kappa
, define the
evaluation map

\displaystyle ev^{ij}_{I}:[0,1]\times\Omega(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})\to M\times M,

\displaystyle ev^{ij}_{I}(\theta,\bm{\gamma})=(\gamma_{i}(\theta),\gamma_{j}(\theta)),

where the subscript
I
stands for
[0,1]
. We then set

\Delta^{ij}_{I}=(ev^{ij}_{I})^{-1}(\Delta_{M}).

The restriction of each
ev^{ij}_{I}
to
[0,1]\times\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
is clearly a
C^{m-1}
-submersion and therefore each
\Delta^{ij}_{I}
restricted to
[0,1]\times\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
is a closed
C^{m-1}
-smooth Banach submanifold of
[0,1]\times\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
of codimension
n
. Note that the loss of regularity is due to taking derivatives in the
\theta
-direction. By abuse of notation, the restriction of
\Delta^{ij}_{I}
to
[0,1]\times\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
will also be denoted
\Delta^{ij}_{I}
, and the ambient space will either be specified or be clear from the context.

Let
\Delta_{I}
be the union
\bigcup_{1\leq i<j\leq\kappa}\Delta^{ij}_{I}
. As before, the subset
\Delta_{I}
(as well as
\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
) decomposes into
\kappa!
connected components corresponding to the permutation types:

\Delta_{I}=\bigsqcup_{\sigma\in S_{\kappa}}\Delta_{I,\sigma}.

In the case
\kappa=2
, the naive definition of the
switching map
from
\Delta_{I}
to itself would be:

(2.4)

(\theta,\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\mapsto(\theta,\gamma_{1}|_{[0,\theta]}\#\gamma_{2}|_{[\theta,1]},\gamma_{2}|_{[0,\theta]}\#\gamma_{1}|_{[\theta,1]}),

where
\#
is concatenation (see Figure
2.1
). This naive switching map, when restricted to
[0,1]\times\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
, unfortunately does not preserve the
C^{m-1}
-smoothness of both concatenated paths at
\theta
and hence cannot have image in
\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
. As a remedy for this, we will apply a certain reparametrization of
[0,1]
which guarantees the
C^{m-1}
-smoothness at
\theta
, i.e., before the switching we replace
(\theta,\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})
by
(\theta,\gamma_{1}\circ\nu,\gamma_{2}\circ\nu)
, where
\nu\colon[0,1]\to[0,1]
is a smooth bijection with all partial derivatives vanishing at
\theta
and satisfying
\nu(t)=t
on most of
[0,1]
. This is a common trick in string topology and appears for example in
[
CJ02
,
Iri18
]
.

The actual implementation of the reparametrization will occupy the next two subsections.

2.2.1.
Multidiagonals

For strata consistency, we need to define switching maps on multidiagonals.

We first consider the simplest case which is the space

(2.5)

\overline{\Delta}_{I_{1}}\cup\overline{\Delta}_{I_{2}}\subset[0,1]^{2}\times\Omega(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime}),

where
\Delta_{I_{1}}\subset\operatorname{Conf}_{2}([0,1])\times\Omega(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
is the preimage of the diagonal
\Delta_{M}
under the evaluations with respect to the first
[0,1]
-coordinate,
\Delta_{I_{2}}
is the preimage under the evaluations with respect to the second
[0,1]
-coordinate,
\overline{\Delta}_{I_{1}}
and
\overline{\Delta}_{I_{2}}
are their closures, and
\operatorname{Conf}_{2}([0,1])
is the
ordered
configuration space of points in
[0,1]
. Note that
\Delta_{I_{i}}
and
\overline{\Delta}_{I_{i}}
are
C^{m-1}
-smooth Banach submanifolds as before when considered inside
[0,1]^{2}\times\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
.

We also define subsets
\Delta^{ij,kl}_{I_{1},I_{2}}\subset\Delta^{ij}_{I_{1}}\cup\Delta^{kl}_{I_{2}}
(here
\{i,j\}=\{k,l\}
is allowed), given by

(2.6)

\displaystyle\Delta^{ij,kl}_{I_{1},I_{2}}=(ev^{ij,kl}_{I_{1},I_{2}})^{-1}(\Delta_{M}\times\Delta_{M}),

where

(2.7)

\displaystyle ev^{ij,kl}_{I_{1},I_{2}}\colon\operatorname{Conf}_{2}([0,1])\times\Omega(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})\to M^{4},

\displaystyle(\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\bm{\gamma})\mapsto(\gamma_{i}(\theta_{1}),\gamma_{j}(\theta_{1}),\gamma_{k}(\theta_{2}),\gamma_{l}(\theta_{2})).

We write

(2.8)

\overline{\Delta}_{I_{1},I_{2}}=\bigcup_{i<j;k<l}\overline{\Delta}^{ij,kl}_{I_{1},I_{2}}\subset[0,1]^{2}\times\Omega(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime}).

The restrictions of the spaces above to
[0,1]^{2}\times\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
are also denoted in the same way by abuse of notation.

Lemma 2.3
.

(1)

The subset
\Delta^{ij,ij}_{I_{1},I_{2}}
is a not necessarily closed
C^{m-1}
-submanifold of
[0,1]^{2}\times\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
of codimension
2n
. The boundary of its closure
\overline{\Delta}^{ij,ij}_{I_{1},I_{2}}
is a
C^{m-2}
-submanifold of codimension
2n+1
for
m\geq 3
.

(2)

For
\{i,j\}\neq\{k,l\}
the subset
\Delta^{ij,kl}_{I_{1},I_{2}}
and its closure
\overline{\Delta}^{ij,kl}_{I_{1},I_{2}}
in
[0,1]^{2}\times\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
are
C^{m-1}
-submanifolds of
[0,1]^{2}\times\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
.

Proof.

(1) It is clear that
ev^{ij,ij}_{I_{1},I_{2}}
is transverse to
\Delta_{M}\times\Delta_{M}
since the strands
\gamma_{i}
and
\gamma_{j}
can be moved independently at
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
. Hence
\Delta^{ij,ij}_{I_{1},I_{2}}
is a codimension
2n
submanifold of
\operatorname{Conf}_{2}([0,1])\times\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
. The boundary of
\Delta^{ij,ij}_{I_{1},I_{2}}\subset[0,1]^{2}\times\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
is a
C^{m-2}
-submanifold of codimension
2n+1
, as it is equal to the
preimage of
\Delta_{TM}
under the submersion from
\Delta_{[0,1]}\times\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
to
TM
given by

(\theta,\theta,\bm{\gamma})\mapsto(\gamma_{i}(\theta),\dot{\gamma}_{i}(\theta),\gamma_{j}(\theta),\dot{\gamma}_{j}(\theta)).

(2) Similarly,
ev^{ij,kl}_{I_{1},I_{2}}
is transverse to
\Delta_{M}\times\Delta_{M}
and the extension of
ev^{ij,kl}_{I_{1},I_{2}}
to
[0,1]^{2}\times\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
is also transverse to
\Delta_{M}\times\Delta_{M}
, since one of the strands, say
\gamma_{l}
, can be moved independently of
\gamma_{i}
and
\gamma_{j}
at the common value
\theta
.
∎

In the general case, given an integer
\ell\geq 2
, a nonempty subset

A=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\}\subseteq[\ell]\coloneqq\{1,\dots,\ell\},

and an
r
-tuple of two-element subsets

\bm{\tau}=(\tau_{1}=\{j^{1}_{1},j^{2}_{1}\},\dots,\tau_{r}=\{j^{1}_{r},j^{2}_{r}\}),

of
[\kappa]
, we define the multi-evaluation map

(2.9)

ev^{\bm{\tau}}_{I_{A}}=ev^{\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{r}}_{I_{i_{1}},\dots,I_{i_{r}}}\colon\operatorname{Conf}_{\ell}([0,1])\times\Omega(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})\to M^{2r},

obtained by composing the projection

\displaystyle\operatorname{Conf}_{\ell}([0,1])\times\Omega(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})\to\operatorname{Conf}_{r}([0,1])\times\Omega(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime}),

\displaystyle(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{\ell},\bm{\gamma})\mapsto(\theta_{i_{1}},\dots,\theta_{i_{r}},\bm{\gamma}),

and the evaluation map

\displaystyle\operatorname{Conf}_{r}([0,1])\times\Omega(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})\to M^{2r},

\displaystyle(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{r},\bm{\gamma})\mapsto(\gamma_{j_{1}^{1}}(\theta_{1}),\gamma_{j_{1}^{2}}(\theta_{1}),\dots,\gamma_{j_{r}^{1}}(\theta_{r}),\gamma_{j_{r}^{2}}(\theta_{r})),

by analogy with (
2.7
). Then the multidiagonal

(2.10)

\Delta_{I_{A}}^{\bm{\tau}}=\Delta_{I_{i_{1}},\dots,I_{i_{r}}}^{\bm{\tau}}\coloneqq(ev^{\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{r}}_{I_{i_{1}},\dots,I_{i_{r}}})^{-1}\Delta_{M}^{\times r},

viewed as a subset of
\operatorname{Conf}_{\ell}([0,1])\times\Omega(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
, is a
C^{m-1}
-submanifold of codimension
\ell n
.

By taking unions of such submanifolds and their closures over various choices of
\bm{\tau}
we define subsets
\Delta_{I_{A}}=\Delta_{I_{i_{1}},\dots,I_{i_{r}}},\overline{\Delta}_{I_{A}}=\overline{\Delta}_{I_{i_{1}},\dots,I_{i_{r}}}\subset[0,1]^{\ell}\times\Omega(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
.
Note that when we write
\Delta_{I_{i_{1}},\dots,I_{i_{r}}}
, the set
[\ell]
is implicit.

2.2.2.
Construction of reparametrizations

We start by considering the
2
-variable case. In this case we pick a smooth family of smooth bijective functions
f_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}}\colon[0,1]\to[0,1]
parametrized by
(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\in\operatorname{Conf}_{2}((0,1))

such that for all
(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\in\operatorname{Conf}_{2}((0,1))
:

(2.11)

\displaystyle f_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}}(\theta_{1})=\theta_{1},\quad f_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}}(\theta_{2})=\theta_{2};

(2.12)

\displaystyle f_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}}^{(k)}(\theta_{1})=f_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}}^{(k)}(\theta_{2})=0\quad\text{ for all }k\geq 1;

(2.13)

\displaystyle f_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}}\text{ continuously extends to the diagonal }\theta_{1}=\theta_{2}.

Here we present an explicit construction of such a family:
Let
\varphi\colon[0,1]\to[0,1]
be a smooth monotonically increasing function such that
\varphi(0)=0
,
\varphi(1)=1
,
\varphi(x)=x
for
x\leq 1-\varepsilon
with
\varepsilon>0
small, and all the derivatives of
\varphi
vanish at
x=1
. We also set
\varphi^{\circ}(x):=1-\varphi(1-x)
. Additionally, let
\psi\colon[0,1]\to[0,1]
be a smooth step function such that
\psi(0)=0
,
\psi(1)=1
, and all the derivatives of
\psi
vanish at the endpoints
x=0,1
(one can take the composition of
\varphi
and
\varphi^{\circ}
, for instance).
We then define
f_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}}
on
\{\theta_{1}\leq\theta_{2}\}
as follows:

(2.14)

f_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\varphi(\frac{x}{\theta_{1}})\cdot\theta_{1},&\mbox{ for }x\leq\theta_{1},\\
\psi(\frac{x-\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}-\theta_{1}})\cdot(\theta_{2}-\theta_{1})+\theta_{1},&\mbox{ for }\theta_{1}<x\leq\theta_{2},\\
\varphi^{\circ}(\frac{x-\theta_{2}}{1-\theta_{2}})\cdot(1-\theta_{2})+\theta_{2},&\mbox{ for }\theta_{2}<x\leq 1.\end{array}\right.

We then extend this function to all of
(0,1)^{2}
by an analogous formula for
\theta_{1}>\theta_{2}
, switching the roles of
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
.
This family is clearly smooth on
\operatorname{Conf}_{2}((0,1))
and extends continuously to all of
(0,1)^{2}
, and hence satisfies
(
2.11
)-(
2.13
). We also set
f_{\theta}:=f_{\theta,\theta}
, which is a smooth family of functions parametrized by the interval
(0,1)
.

We can generalize the above construction to the
r
-variable case, i.e., to a continuous family of smooth monotonically increasing functions
f_{\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{r}}\colon[0,1]\to[0,1]
parametrized by
(0,1)^{r}
, whose restriction to
\operatorname{Conf}_{r}((0,1))
is smooth and satisfies following:

(2.15)

\displaystyle f_{\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{r}}(\theta_{i})=\theta_{i}\quad\text{ for all $i$ such that $1\leq i\leq r$;}

(2.16)

\displaystyle f_{\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{r}}^{(k)}(\theta_{i})=0\quad\text{ for all $k\geq 1$ and $i$ such that $1\leq i\leq r$}.

Such a family can be constructed using a formula similar to (
2.14
).

We also choose a function
\varepsilon_{[r]}:[0,1]^{r}\to(0,\frac{1}{4}]
,
\bm{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{r})\mapsto\varepsilon_{[r]}(\bm{\theta})
, such that
f_{\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{r}}(x)=x
on
\varepsilon_{[r]}(\bm{\theta})
-neighborhoods of
x=0
and
x=1
. The existence of such a function is immediate from the definition of
f_{\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{r}}
.

Given a subset
A=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\}\subset[\ell]
as before, we construct a continuous family of smooth monotone functions
\nu_{A}=\nu_{i_{1},\dots,i_{r}}:[0,1]\to[0,1]
parametrized by
\overline{\Delta}_{i_{1}}\cup\dots\cup\overline{\Delta}_{i_{r}}
: First choose a cover
\{U_{S}\}_{S\subset A}
of
\overline{\Delta}_{i_{1}}\cup\dots\cup\overline{\Delta}_{i_{r}}

such that:

(C1)

\overline{\Delta}_{I_{S}}\subset\bigcup_{S^{\prime}\subseteq S}U_{S^{\prime}}
for all
S\subset A
;

(C2)

U_{S^{\prime}}\cap\overline{\Delta}_{S}=\emptyset
for all pairs
S,S^{\prime}\subset A
such that
S^{\prime}\subsetneq S
.

Fix a partition of unity
\{\psi_{S}\}_{S\subset A}
subordinate to
\{U_{S}\}_{S\subset A}
. Given
S=\{j_{1},\dots,j_{r^{\prime}}\}\subset A
we define

f_{S}:U_{S}\times[0,1]\to[0,1],\quad((\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{\ell},\bm{\gamma}),x)\mapsto f_{\theta_{j_{1}},\dots,\theta_{j_{r^{\prime}}}}(x).

We then set

(2.17)

\nu_{A}=\sum_{S\subset A}\psi_{S}f_{S}.

By (C1) and (C2), the restriction of
\nu_{A}
to
\overline{\Delta}_{S}
is equal to
f_{S}
on the complement
of the union
\bigcup_{S^{\prime}\subsetneq S}U_{S^{\prime}}
.
The function
\nu_{[\ell]}
can then be extended to all of
[0,1]^{\ell}\times\Omega(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
by adding to the cover
\{U_{S}\}_{S\subset[\ell]}
an open set
U_{\emptyset}
which does not intersect any of the
\overline{\Delta}_{S}
.

We also choose a smooth function

\varepsilon_{[\ell]}:[0,1]^{\ell}\times\Omega(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})\to(0,\tfrac{1}{4}]

such that
\nu_{[\ell]}(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{\ell},\bm{\gamma})=\operatorname{id}
(whenever it is defined) in the
\varepsilon_{[\ell]}(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{\ell},\bm{\gamma})
-neighborhoods of
0
and
1
. Such a function can be constructed using functions
\varepsilon_{\{j_{1},\dots,j_{r^{\prime}}\}}
and we leave the details to the reader.

2.2.3.
Switching

Definition 2.4
.

For
\overline{\Delta}^{ij}_{I_{k}}\subset[0,1]^{\times\ell}\times\Omega(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
we define the switching map
sw_{I_{k}}^{ij}\colon\overline{\Delta}^{ij}_{I_{k}}\to\overline{\Delta}^{ij}_{I_{k}}
by the formula

(2.18)

\displaystyle sw_{I_{k}}^{ij}(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{\ell},\bm{\gamma})=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{\ell},\gamma_{1}(\nu_{[\ell]}(\cdot)),\dots,\gamma_{i-1}(\nu_{[\ell]}(\cdot)),\gamma_{i}(\nu_{[\ell]}(\cdot))|_{[0,\theta_{k}]}\#\gamma_{j}(\nu_{[\ell]}(\cdot))|_{[\theta_{k},1]},

\displaystyle\gamma_{i+1}(\nu_{[\ell]}(\cdot)),\dots,\gamma_{j-1}(\nu_{[\ell]}(\cdot)),\gamma_{j}(\nu_{[\ell]}(\cdot))|_{[0,\theta_{k}]}\#\gamma_{i}(\nu_{[\ell]}(\cdot))|_{[\theta_{k},1]},\gamma_{j+1}(\nu_{[\ell]}(\cdot)),\dots,\gamma_{\kappa}(\nu_{[\ell]}(\cdot))).

Here
\#
stands for the standard concatenation. The maps
sw_{I_{k}}^{ij}
are
C^{m-1}
-smooth when restricted to
\Delta_{I_{k}}\subset[0,1]\times\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
. They are not
C^{m-1}
-smooth on all of
\overline{\Delta}_{I_{k}}
for the same reason that
f_{\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{\ell}}
is not smooth along diagonals
\theta_{i}=\theta_{j}
. Nevertheless, there are some additional loci inside
\overline{\Delta}_{I_{k}}
where the map still has a certain level of regularity.

Explicitly, let
S_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup S_{r}=[\ell]
be a partition of
[\ell]
and associate to it a subset
\Delta_{S_{1},\dots,S_{r}}([0,1]^{\ell})
of
[0,1]^{\ell}
consisting of points
(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{\ell})
such that
\theta_{i}=\theta_{j}
if and only if
i
and
j
belong to the same element of the partition.
Then it is immediate from the definition of
\nu_{[\ell]}
that each map
sw_{I_{k}}^{ij}
is
C^{m-1}
smooth when restricted to the intersection of
\overline{\Delta}_{I_{k}}
with
\Delta_{S_{1},\dots,S_{r}}([0,1]^{\ell})\times\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
for any partition
S_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup S_{r}
. The main case of interest for us is the one where there are exactly
\ell-1
elements in the partition, i.e., exactly two coordinates are allowed to be equal.

Figure 2.1
.
Here we schematically illustrate the effect of the switching map
sw^{ij}_{I_{1}}
on the left tuple of paths, where we have locally drawn
3
out of
\kappa
paths. All paths except
\gamma_{i}
and
\gamma_{j}
are unaffected, and the portion of
\gamma_{i}
after the intersection point at
\theta
gets replaced with the reparametrized portion of
\gamma_{j}
and vice versa. Dashed lines depict the portions of
\gamma_{i}
and
\gamma_{j}
affected by the smoothing reparametrization.

Remark 2.5
.

The naive switching map differs from the above switching map only in the absence of reparametrizations governed by
\nu
, so the images of both maps are identical. The following proposition justifies the introduction of the reparametrizations.
The reason for the complicated construction involving a cover of
\overline{\Delta}_{I_{\ell}}
satisfying (C1) and (C2) will become clear in the proof of Theorem
C.5
and one should understand that on most of
\overline{\Delta}_{I_{k}}
the reparametrization
\nu
is controlled by the function
f_{\theta_{k}}
.

Proposition 2.6
.

For arbitrary indices
p_{1},p_{2}\in[\ell]
:

(1)

the map
sw^{ij}_{I_{p_{1}}}
is a
C^{m-1}
-immersion when restricted to
\Delta_{S_{1},\dots,S_{r}}\times\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
for an arbitrary partition
S_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup S_{r}=[\ell]
;

(2)

the following commutativity relations hold:

(2.19)

\displaystyle sw^{ij}_{I_{p_{2}}}(sw^{ij}_{I_{p_{1}}}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\gamma}))=sw^{ij}_{I_{p_{1}}}(sw^{ij}_{I_{p_{2}}}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\gamma})),\text{ if }(\bm{\theta},\bm{\gamma})\in\Delta^{ij,ij}_{I_{p_{1}},I_{p_{2}}},

(2.20)

\displaystyle sw^{jk}_{I_{p_{2}}}(sw^{ij}_{I_{p_{1}}}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\gamma}))=sw^{ij}_{I_{p_{1}}}(sw^{ik}_{I_{p_{2}}}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\gamma})),\text{ if }(\bm{\theta},\bm{\gamma})\in\Delta^{ij,ik}_{I_{p_{1}},I_{p_{2}}}\text{ and }\theta_{p_{1}}\leq\theta_{p_{2}},

(2.21)

\displaystyle sw^{ik}_{I_{p_{2}}}(sw^{ij}_{I_{p_{1}}}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\gamma}))=sw^{kj}_{I_{p_{1}}}(sw^{ik}_{I_{p_{2}}}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\gamma})),\text{ if }(\bm{\theta},\bm{\gamma})\in\Delta^{ij,ik}_{I_{p_{1}},I_{p_{2}}}\text{ and }\theta_{p_{1}}\geq\theta_{p_{2}},

(2.22)

\displaystyle sw_{I_{p_{1}}}^{ij}(sw^{kl}_{I_{p_{2}}}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\gamma}))=sw_{I_{p_{2}}}^{ij}(sw_{I_{p_{1}}}^{kl}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\gamma}))\text{ if }(\bm{\theta},\bm{\gamma})\in\Delta^{ij,kl}_{I_{p_{1}},I_{p_{2}}},

where
i,j,k,l
are assumed to be distinct and
\bm{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{\ell})
.

Proof.

(1) is straightforward from the previous discussion.

(2) The first relation (
2.19
) follows from observing that for any
(\bm{\theta},\bm{\gamma})\in\Delta^{ij,ij}_{I_{p_{1}},I_{p_{2}}}
the reparametrizations
\nu_{\bm{\theta},\bm{\gamma}}
involved in the definition of both
sw_{I_{p_{1}}}^{ij}
and
sw_{I_{p_{2}}}^{ij}
are the same. The other relations are similar and left to the reader.
∎

We additionally point out that the closure of
\Delta_{I_{1},\dots,I_{\ell}}^{\bm{\tau}}
inside
[0,1]^{\ell}\times\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
is a subset of codimension at least
\ell n
stratified by finitely many
C^{m-\ell}
-submanifolds (most of them have higher regularity).

We consider the case where all the
\tau_{s}
agree with a fixed
\{i,j\}
in more detail. The main observation here is that, when
k
intersection points of
\gamma_{i}
and
\gamma_{j}
collide at one point, all first
k
partial derivatives of these two paths agree at the given point. This condition cuts out a
C^{m-k}
-submanifold by considering a natural evaluation map which takes values in the
k
th jet bundle of
TM
. Each partition
(k_{1},\dots,k_{r})
of
\ell
corresponds to a stratum where
k_{s}
intersection points of
\gamma_{i}
and
\gamma_{j}
collide for all
s=1,\dots,r
. There are
B_{\ell}
such strata in this case, where
B_{\ell}
is the
\ell
-th Bell number. For a general
\bm{\tau}
,
B_{\ell}
gives an upper bound for the number of strata.

2.3.
The based multiloop complex

In this paper, we use
cohomologically graded
Morse chain complexes.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be set of the critical points of
\mathscr{A}=\mathscr{A}_{L}
, which are given by the Euler-Lagrange equation

(2.23)

\frac{d}{dt}\partial_{v}L(t,\gamma(t),\dot{\gamma}(t))=\partial_{x}L(t,\gamma(t),\dot{\gamma}(t)).

To each critical point
\gamma\in\mathscr{P}
we associate a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded rank
1
free abelian group
o_{\gamma}
, called
orientation line
, whose
\mathbb{Z}
-grading is equal to that of
\gamma
(with negative Morse grading). Then for
\kappa=1
the graded
\mathbb{Z}
-module
CM_{-*}(\Omega^{1,2}(M,q))
is the direct sum
\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\mathscr{P}}o_{\gamma}
.
The Morse differential is given by counting index
1

perturbed negative gradient trajectories

(2.24)

\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial s}=X(\Gamma),

where we are parametrizing paths of
\Omega^{1,2}(M,q)
by
s
and
X\in\mathscr{G}
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime};X)
be the moduli space of perturbed negative gradient trajectories from
\gamma
to
\gamma^{\prime}
with respect to
X\in\mathscr{G}
and
\mathscr{M}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime};X)=\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime};X)/\mathbb{R}
be the quotient by
\mathbb{R}
-translation. To each
\Gamma\in\mathscr{M}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime};X)
there is an associated map between orientation lines

\partial_{\Gamma}\colon o_{\gamma}\to o_{\gamma}^{\prime},

whenever the moduli space of such trajectories is
0
-dimensional.
Then we define

(2.25)

\displaystyle d\colon CM_{-*}(\Omega^{1,2}(M,q))\to CM_{-*}(\Omega^{1,2}(M,q))

\displaystyle d[\gamma]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P},\Gamma\in\mathscr{M}^{\operatorname{ind}=1}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime};X)\end{subarray}}\partial_{\Gamma}([\gamma]),

where the superscript
\operatorname{ind}=1
refers to the virtual dimension of
\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime};X)
and
[\gamma]
is some generator of
o_{\gamma}
. The vector field
X
will be suppressed when it is understood or is unimportant.

For
\kappa\geq 1
and
\bm{\gamma}\in\Omega^{1,2}_{\sigma}(M,\bm{q})
, there is a straightforward definition of a perturbed negative gradient trajectory on
\Omega_{\sigma}^{1,2}(M,\bm{q})
given by

(2.26)

\frac{\partial\bm{\Gamma}}{\partial s}={\bm{X}}(\bm{\Gamma})\coloneqq\prod_{i=1}^{\kappa}{X^{i}}(\Gamma_{i}),

where
{\bm{X}}=(X^{1},\dots,X^{\kappa})
and
X^{i}\in\mathscr{G}
for
\Omega^{1,2}(M,q_{i},q_{\sigma(i)})
. By abuse of notation we write
{\bm{X}}=(X^{1},\dots,X^{\kappa})\in\mathscr{G}^{\kappa}
with the understanding that each
X^{i}
depends on
q_{i}
and
\sigma
; if
\bm{\gamma}\in\Omega^{1,2}(M,{\bm{q}})
where
\sigma
is unspecified, then we assume that
\bm{X}
is with respect to the appropriate
\sigma
.
The Morse cohomology whose differential is defined by counting gradient multi-trajectories between critical points as above gives nothing but
\kappa
copies of the Morse (co)homology
HM_{-*}(\Omega^{1,2}(M,q))
of the based loop space of
M
tensored with the symmetric group
S_{\kappa}
.
In order to include a graded version of the HOMFLY skein relation, we need to modify the definition of the Morse differential so that, given a gradient trajectory
\bm{\Gamma}(s)
starting at
\bm{\gamma}
(i.e., satisfying Equation (
2.26
)), whenever there exist
s,t
such that
\bm{\Gamma}(s)(t)
crosses the big diagonal of
\mathrm{Sym}^{\kappa}(M)
, additional trajectories that bifurcate from the original one are created.

Specifically, denote the set of critical points of
\mathscr{A}
on
\Omega_{\sigma}^{1,2}(M,\bm{q})
by
\mathscr{P}_{\sigma}
and (abusing notation) the collection of all such multiloops by

\mathscr{P}=\bigsqcup_{\sigma\in S_{\kappa}}\mathscr{P}_{\sigma}.

To
\bm{\gamma}=(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{\kappa})\in\mathscr{P}
we associate the orientation line

o_{\bm{\gamma}}=o_{\gamma_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes o_{\gamma_{\kappa}}.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
CM_{0,-*}(\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q}))
be the
R=\mathbb{Z}
-module generated by the orientation lines associated with elements of
\mathscr{P}
. Then the based multiloop complex
CM_{-*}(\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q}))
of
M
is a cochain complex whose underlying module is
CM_{0,-*}(\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q}))\llbracket\hbar\rrbracket
when
n=2
and
CM_{0,-*}(\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q}))\otimes_{R}R[\hbar]
when
n>2
. The differential is given by Equation (
2.30
) below.

In Appendix
B.1
we introduce spaces of perturbations of vector fields
\mathfrak{X}_{-},\mathfrak{X}_{+},\mathfrak{X}_{0}
for any positive integer
m
. Let us fix
m
sufficiently large (
m\geq 3
suffices). The elements of these spaces are certain maps to the space of vector fields on
\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime})
from domains
(-\infty,0],[0,+\infty)
and
[0,+\infty)^{2}
, respectively.

Let
\ell\geq 0
be an integer,
\bm{\tau}=(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{\ell})
an
\ell
-tuple of pairs
\tau_{1}=\{i_{1},j_{1}\},\dots,\tau_{\ell}=\{i_{\ell},j_{\ell}\}
of elements of
[\kappa]
, and

\bm{Y}=(Y_{0},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{\ell-1},Y_{\ell})\in\mathfrak{X}_{-}\times(\mathfrak{X}_{0})^{\ell-1}\times\mathfrak{X}_{+}

an
(\ell+1)
-tuple of perturbations. We will also need an ordering/permutation
\bm{s}=(s_{1},\dots,s_{\ell})
of elements of
[\ell]
. (In the following definition, the first switch that occurs is labeled
s_{1}
, the second switch is labeled
s_{2}
, etc.)

Definition 2.8
.

Given two critical multiloops
\bm{\gamma}\in\mathscr{P}_{\sigma}
,
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}_{\sigma^{\prime}}
, a multiloop flow line with switchings (abbreviated MFLS) from
\bm{\gamma}
to
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
with respect to data
\ell,\bm{Y},\bm{\tau}
,
\bm{s}
,
2
2

2

More precisely, it’s a “multiloop flow line with possible switchings”, but that would be too cumbersome.

is a tuple

\bm{\Gamma}=\left((\theta_{s_{1}},\Gamma_{0}),(\theta_{s_{2}},\Gamma_{1},l_{1}),\dots,(\theta_{s_{\ell}},\Gamma_{\ell-1},l_{\ell-1}),\Gamma_{\ell}\right),

where
\bm{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{\ell})\in\operatorname{Conf}_{\ell}([0,1])
,
l_{1},\dots,l_{\ell-1}\in[0;+\infty)
and the following hold:

(1)

The maps

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Gamma_{0}\colon(-\infty,0]\to\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q});\\
\Gamma_{i}\colon[0,l_{i}]\to\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q})\quad\text{ for }\quad i=1,\dots,\ell-1;\\
\Gamma_{\ell}\colon[0,+\infty)\to\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q})\end{array}\right.

are continuously differentiable.

(2)

\Gamma_{i}^{\prime}(s)={(\bm{X}+Y_{i}(l_{i},s))}_{\Gamma_{i}(s)}
for
\,i=0,\dots,\ell
(by definition, for
i=0,\ell
,
Y_{i}(l_{i},s)
is simply
Y_{i}(s)
).

(3)

\Gamma_{0}(-\infty)=\bm{\gamma}
,
\Gamma_{\ell}(+\infty)=\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
.

(4)

For
k=1,\dots,\ell
,
(\bm{\theta},\Gamma_{k-1}(l_{k-1}))\in\Delta^{\tau_{k}}_{I_{s_{k}}}
(here we are taking
l_{0}=0
) and

sw^{i_{k}j_{k}}_{I_{s_{k}}}(\bm{\theta},\Gamma_{k}(l_{k}))=(\bm{\theta},\Gamma_{k+1}(0)).

Let
\mathscr{M}_{\ell}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\bm{Y},\bm{\tau},\bm{s})
be the moduli space of all MFLS
\bm{\gamma}
from
\bm{\gamma}
to
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
with respect to
\ell,\bm{Y},\bm{\tau},\bm{s}
.

Notation 2.9
.

We often write
\bm{\Gamma}=(\Gamma_{0},\dots,\Gamma_{\ell})
, suppressing the
\theta_{i}
and
l_{i}
from the notation. We also often omit the subscript
\ell
from
\mathscr{M}_{\ell}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\bm{Y},\bm{\tau},\bm{s})
since
|\bm{\tau}|=\ell
.

Figure 2.2
.
Here we depict the perturbation scheme for an MFLS with
3
switches, marked by red crosses. Away from the location of switching markers the trajectories are the flow lines of the fixed pseudogradient vector field
X
. Near the switches they are flow lines of the perturbed vector fields
X+Y_{i}
. Note that
Y_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,\ell-1
is nonzero near the
i
th and
(i+1)
st switching markers as depicted.

We write
\operatorname{ind}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime},\ell)
for the virtual dimension of
\mathscr{M}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\bm{Y},\bm{\tau},\bm{s})
(or
\operatorname{ind}(\bm{\Gamma})
if
\bm{\Gamma}\in\mathscr{M}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\bm{Y},\bm{\tau},\bm{s})
), which is equal to
-(n-2)\ell+|\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}|-|\bm{\gamma}|
by Theorem
B.6
. The grading of
\bm{\gamma}=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{\kappa})\in\mathscr{P}
is
cohomological
, i.e.,
|\bm{\gamma}|=|\gamma_{1}|+\dots+|\gamma_{\kappa}|
, where
|\gamma_{i}|=-\operatorname{ind}(\gamma_{i})
and
\operatorname{ind}
is the Morse index. The moduli spaces
\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\bm{Y},\bm{\tau},\bm{s})
are oriented as in
[
KY23
]
.

Lemma 2.10
.

For generic
\bm{Y}\in\mathfrak{X}_{-}\times(\mathfrak{X}_{0})^{\ell-1}\times\mathfrak{X}_{+}
, given
\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}
,
\ell
,
\bm{\tau}
and
\bm{s}
:

(1)

\mathscr{M}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\bm{Y},\bm{\tau},\bm{s})
is a smooth manifold of dimension

\operatorname{dim}\mathscr{M}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\bm{Y},\bm{\tau},\bm{s})=\operatorname{ind}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime},\ell)=-(n-2)\ell+|\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}|-|\bm{\gamma}|;

in particular, if
\operatorname{dim}\mathscr{M}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\bm{Y},\bm{\tau},\bm{s})
is negative, then
\mathscr{M}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\bm{Y},\bm{\tau},\bm{s})=\varnothing
.

(2)

If
\operatorname{dim}\mathscr{M}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\bm{Y},\bm{\tau},\bm{s})=0
, then
\mathscr{M}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\bm{Y},\bm{\tau},\bm{s})
is finite.

(3)

There exists a
consistent choice of perturbation data

\{\bm{Y}^{\ell}_{\bm{\tau},\bm{s}}\}_{\ell\geq 0,|\bm{\tau}|=\ell,\bm{s}}
, where

\bm{Y}^{\ell}_{\tau,\bm{s}}=(Y^{\ell}_{\tau,\bm{s},0},\dots,Y^{\ell}_{\tau,\bm{s},\ell})\in\mathfrak{X}_{-}\times(\mathfrak{X}_{0})^{\ell-1}\times\mathfrak{X}_{+},

such that

\bigcup_{\bm{s}\in S_{\ell},\bm{\tau}\colon|\bm{\tau}|=\ell}\mathscr{M}^{\operatorname{ind}=1}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\bm{Y}^{\ell}_{\bm{\tau},\bm{s}},\bm{\tau},\bm{s})

admits a compactification with boundary strata of the form

(2.27)

\mathscr{M}^{\operatorname{ind}=0}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime\prime};\bm{Y}^{\ell^{\prime}}_{\bm{\tau}^{\prime},\bm{s}^{\prime}},\bm{\tau}^{\prime},\bm{s}^{\prime})\times\mathscr{M}^{\operatorname{ind}=0}(\bm{\gamma}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\bm{Y}^{\ell-\ell^{\prime}}_{\bm{\tau}^{\prime\prime},\bm{s}^{\prime\prime}},\bm{\tau}^{\prime\prime},\bm{s}^{\prime\prime}),

where
\bm{\tau}=(\bm{\tau}^{\prime},\bm{\tau}^{\prime\prime})
,
|\bm{\tau}^{\prime}|=\ell^{\prime}\geq 0
,
|\bm{\tau}^{\prime\prime}|=\ell-\ell^{\prime}\geq 0
, and
\bm{s}^{\prime}
is the ordering of the elements of
[\ell^{\prime}]
by considering the order on the first
\ell^{\prime}
elements of
\bm{s}
, while
\bm{s}^{\prime\prime}
is a similarly defined ordering on
[\ell-\ell^{\prime}]
.

(4)

Fixing a choice of orientations on unstable manifolds of
\bm{\gamma}
and
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
provides a natural orientation of
\mathscr{M}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\bm{Y},\bm{\tau},\bm{s})
, which depends on the orientation of
M
.

Proof.

(1) follows from regularity (Theorem
B.6
).

(2) follows from (1) and compactness (Appendix
C
).

(3) is a consequence of Appendix
C
, regularity, and gluing (which is not proven here). We note that the statement here is slightly inaccurate and we refer to Theorem
C.5
for a precise statement.

(4) is analogous to
[
KY23
, Proposition 3.4]
and will be omitted.
∎

Remark 2.11
.

We may alternatively use
CM_{-*}(\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime}))
instead of
CM_{-*}(\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q}))
, where
{\bm{q}}^{\prime}
is a perturbation of
{\bm{q}}
. The proof of Lemma
2.10
remains the same.

Figure 2.3
.
A schematic depiction of an MFLS with
2
switchings. As before the red crosses denote switches and the thick blue segments denote the perturbed parts of the trajectories.

By Lemma
2.10
(4), to any MFLS
\bm{\Gamma}\in\mathscr{M}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\bm{Y},\bm{\tau},\bm{s})
with
\operatorname{ind}(\bm{\Gamma})=0
there is an associated map of orientation lines

\partial_{\bm{\Gamma}}\colon o_{\bm{\gamma}}\to o_{\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}}[(n-2)\ell].

For a generator
[\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}]\in o_{\bm{\gamma}}
we will denote by
[\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}]\hbar^{\ell}
the corresponding generator of
o_{\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}}[(n-2)\ell]
, where
\hbar
is our Planck constant with grading
|\hbar|=2-n
. We use the convention

(2.28)

\displaystyle o[-k]\cong o\otimes\zeta^{k},\,k>0;

(2.29)

\displaystyle o[k]\cong\zeta^{-k}\otimes o,\,k>0.

where
o
is any orientation line and
\zeta
is the trivial orientation line of grading
1
.

For a universal (i.e., consistent) choice of perturbation data
\{\bm{Y}^{\ell}_{\bm{\tau},\bm{s}}\}_{\ell\geq 0,|\bm{\tau}|=\ell,\bm{s}}
as in Lemma
2.10
, the based multiloop differential is defined as follows:

(2.30)

\displaystyle\mu^{1}_{M}([\bm{\gamma}])=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{\gamma}^{\prime},\,\ell\geq 0,\bm{\tau},\bm{s}\\
\bm{\Gamma}\in\mathscr{M}^{\operatorname{ind}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime},\ell)=0}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\bm{Y}^{\ell}_{\bm{\tau},\bm{s}},\bm{\tau},\bm{s})\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|\bm{\gamma}|}\frac{\partial_{\bm{\Gamma}}([\bm{\gamma}])}{\ell!},

where
[\bm{\gamma}]
is a generator of
o_{\bm{\gamma}}
.

Remark 2.12
.

Note that the count above is integer despite dividing by
\ell!
, because we can also make sure that the perturbation data is symmetric; see Appendix
C
.

Lemma 2.13
.

For a choice of universal perturbation data
\{\bm{Y}^{\ell}_{\bm{\tau},\bm{s}}\}_{\ell\geq 0,|\bm{\tau}|=\ell,\bm{s}}
as in Lemma
2.10
the differential
\mu^{1}_{M}
is well-defined and
\mu^{1}_{M}\circ\mu^{1}_{M}=0
.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of Lemma
2.10
.
∎

Example 2.14
.

Let
M=S^{2}
be the unit
2
-sphere with the round metric induced from
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
Pick basepoints
\bm{q}=(q_{1},q_{2})
where
\{q_{1},q_{2}\}\subset S^{2}
and
q_{1}\neq q_{2}
.
Let
x_{1}
be the tuple of geodesics of total degree
-1
which consists of an equator
\gamma_{1}
of degree
-1
based at
q_{1}
and the constant loop
\gamma_{2}
at
q_{2}
. Our convention is such that the orientation of the unstable manifold of a geodesic path of degree
-1
coincides with the orientation of the trajectory from such a path to
1
contained in the
right
hemisphere with respect to the path. With respect to this convention
[x_{1}]\in CM_{-*}(S^{2},\bm{q})
is the corresponding generator and by abuse of notation we also denote it by
x_{1}=[x_{1}]
.

There are two gradient trajectories from
\gamma_{1}
to the constant loop at
q_{1}
. One has no intersection with
q_{2}
while the other one crosses the graph of the constant loop of
q_{2}
transversely. Note that explicit calculation in the closed string case is given in
[
ACF16
, Appendix B]
and the open string case is analogous.
Therefore, there is an MFLS with
1
switching which occurs when the trajectory above reaches
q_{2}
.
This is illustrated in Figure
2.4
.
Hence,

\mu^{1}_{M}(x_{1})=\hbar\,T_{1}+o(\hbar),

where
T_{1}=\{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\}
,
\omega_{1}
is the shortest geodesic from
q_{1}
to
q_{2}
, and
\omega_{2}
is the shortest geodesic from
q_{2}
to
q_{1}
;
o(\hbar)
denotes the higher-order terms in
\hbar
. Here again we abuse notation and denote the generator
[T_{1}]
simply by
T_{1}
. Note that the sign in front of the first term is implied by our orientation convention and the additional sign twist coming from (
2.30
).

Figure 2.4
.

2.4.
The
A_{\infty}
-structure

In this subsection we describe the
A_{\infty}
-structure on the based multiloop complex
CM_{-*}(\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q}))
. The
A_{\infty}
-operations
are given by a signed count of pseudogradient flow trees of the action functional with
\ell
switchings.
See Figure
2.5
for an illustration, where the edges are flow lines with switchings and the vertices represent concatenations.

Figure 2.5
.
Schematic depiction of an MFTS with
2
switchings contributing to
[\bm{\gamma}_{0}]\hbar^{2}
in
\mu^{5}_{M}([\bm{\gamma}_{5}],[\bm{\gamma}_{4}],[\bm{\gamma}_{3}],[\bm{\gamma}_{2}],[\bm{\gamma}_{1}])
. As before we depict in blue the areas where additional perturbations are turned on.

We now give a more precise definition of the
A_{\infty}
-operations. We first make some preliminary definitions.

Let
h_{\varepsilon}:[0,1]\to[0,1]
,
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{4}]
, be a family of smooth step functions such that:

(2.31)

\displaystyle h_{\varepsilon}(0)=0,\quad h_{\varepsilon}(1)=1,\quad h_{\varepsilon}(x)=x\text{\penalty\ \penalty\ for \penalty\ \penalty\ $\varepsilon\leq x\leq 1-\varepsilon$},

(2.32)

\displaystyle h^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(x)>0\text{\penalty\ \penalty\ for \penalty\ \penalty\ $x\in(0,1)$},\quad h_{\varepsilon}^{(k)}(0)=h_{\varepsilon}^{(k)}(1)=0\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall k>0.

The family
\{h_{\varepsilon}\}
is used to smooth out points of concatenation in the following definition:

Definition 2.15

(Weighted concatenation)
.

Let
\bm{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{d})
be a vector of positive real numbers and
\bm{q}_{0},\dots,\bm{q}_{d}
be
\kappa
-tuples of points in
M
. Writing
a=a_{1}+\dots+a_{d}
, the weighted concatenation map is given by:

\displaystyle c^{\bm{a}}:[0,1]^{\ell}\times\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q}_{d-1},\bm{q}_{d})\times\dots\times\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q}_{0},\bm{q}_{1})\to[0,1]^{\ell}\times\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q}_{0},\bm{q}_{d}),

\displaystyle(\bm{\theta},\bm{\gamma}_{d},\dots,\bm{\gamma}_{1})\mapsto\left(\bm{\theta},\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{$\bm{\gamma}_{1}(h_{\varepsilon}(\tfrac{a}{a_{1}}t))$, \hskip 10.22217pt$t\in[0,\tfrac{a_{1}}{a}]$,}\\
\text{$\bm{\gamma}_{2}(h_{\varepsilon}(\tfrac{a}{a_{2}}t-\tfrac{a_{1}}{a_{2}}))$, \hskip 10.22217pt$t\in[\tfrac{a_{1}}{a},\tfrac{a_{1}+a_{2}}{a}]$,}\\
\qquad\vdots\\
\text{$\bm{\gamma}_{d}(h_{\varepsilon}(\tfrac{a}{a_{d}}t-\tfrac{a_{1}+\dots+a_{d-1}}{a_{d}}))$,\hskip 10.22217pt$t\in[\tfrac{a_{1}+\dots+a_{d-1}}{a},\tfrac{a_{1}+\dots+a_{d}}{a}]$}\end{array}\right.\right),

where
\varepsilon>0
is a small constant.

Observe that precomposing with reparametrizations of type
h_{\varepsilon}
guarantees that the image is indeed in
\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q}_{0},\bm{q}_{d})
.

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
(\bm{\theta},\bm{\gamma}_{p})\in\overline{\Delta}_{I_{k}}^{ij}
, where
1\leq p\leq d
,
i\not=j\in[\kappa]
, and
k\in[\ell]
. Then

(2.33)

\displaystyle c^{\bm{a}}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\gamma}_{d},\dots,\bm{\gamma}_{p+1},sw^{ij}_{I_{k}}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\gamma}_{p}),\bm{\gamma}_{p-1},\dots,\bm{\gamma}_{1})=sw^{\tilde{i}\tilde{j}}_{I_{k+\ell_{d}+\dots+\ell_{p+1}}}(c^{\bm{a}}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\gamma}_{d},\dots,\bm{\gamma}_{1})),

where
\tilde{i}
and
\tilde{j}
are obtained by applying
\sigma^{-1}(\bm{\gamma}_{1})\circ\dots\circ\sigma^{-1}(\bm{\gamma}_{p-1})
to
i
and
j
, respectively, and
\sigma(\bm{\gamma})
is the permutation type of the multipath
\bm{\gamma}
.

Proof.

This follows from the definitions of
h_{\epsilon}
and
\nu_{\ell}
.
∎

Definition 2.17
.

A
ribbon tree with
d
inputs and
1
output
is a directed planar tree
T=(V,E)
such that:

(1)

V=V_{\operatorname{int}}\sqcup V_{\operatorname{ext}}
is the set of vertices and
E=E_{\operatorname{int}}\sqcup E_{\operatorname{ext}}
is the set of (directed) edges, where
V_{\operatorname{int}}
(resp.
V_{\operatorname{ext}}
) is the set of interior (resp. exterior) vertices;
V_{\operatorname{ext}}=\{v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{d}\}
and
E_{\operatorname{ext}}=\{e_{0},e_{1},\dots,e_{d}\}
;
v_{1},\dots,v_{d}
are the
incoming vertices
which have
1
outgoing edge each;
v_{0}
is the
outgoing vertex
which has
1
incoming edge;
e_{1},\dots,e_{d}
are the outgoing edges from
v_{1},\dots,v_{d}
; and
e_{0}
is the incoming edge to
v_{0}
;

(2)

every vertex
v\in V_{\operatorname{int}}
has exactly
1
outgoing edge
e^{v}_{0}
; the incoming edges
e^{v}_{1},\dots,e^{v}_{|v|-1}
are cyclically ordered (here
|v|
denotes the valence of
v
) around the vertex in a manner consistent with the planar embedding of
T
(counterclockwise around
v)
; and the exterior vertices
v_{0},\dots,v_{d}
are cyclically ordered in a similar manner.

Given
e\in E
, let us denote its
source
and
target vertices
by
s(e),t(e)\in V
.

Let
\mathscr{T}_{d}
denote the set of isomorphism classes of ribbon trees with
d
inputs and
1
output; we will abuse notation and not distinguish between a ribbon tree and its isomorphism class. For
e\in E_{\operatorname{int}}(T)
we denote by
T/e
the element of
\mathscr{T}_{d}
obtained from
T
by contracting the edge
e
.

We denote by
\mathfrak{T}_{d}
the space of Stasheff (= metric) ribbon trees with
d
inputs and
1
output and refer the reader to
[
Abo09
, Section 4]
for a more in-depth discussion of the topology of these spaces. Here a metric ribbon tree will have infinite length along all the exterior edges and finite length along each interior edge.

Definition 2.18
.

A switching data
\bm{\tau}
on a tree
T\in\mathscr{T}_{d}
assigns:

(1)

to each
e\in E(T)
of an
\ell_{e}
-tuple
\bm{\tau}_{e}
of pairs of distinct elements of
[\kappa]
; and

(2)

to each
v\in V_{\operatorname{int}}(T)
and an incoming edge
e^{v}_{i}
, a tuple
\bm{\tau}_{v,i}
of pairs of distinct elements of
[\kappa]
, such that
\bm{\tau}_{v}=(\bm{\tau}_{v,1},\dots,\bm{\tau}_{v,|v|-1})
has cardinality
\ell_{v}
.

The integers
\ell_{e}
and
\ell_{v}
are the switching numbers on
e
and
v
.

We denote by
\mathscr{T}_{d}^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}
the set of
(T,\bm{\tau})
, where
T\in\mathscr{T}_{d}
and
\bm{\tau}
is a switching data on
T
with
\ell
edge switches and
\ell^{\prime}
vertex switches, i.e.,

(2.34)

\sum_{e\in E(T)}\ell_{e}=\ell,\sum_{v\in V_{\operatorname{int}}(T)}\ell_{v}=\ell^{\prime}.

Given
(T,\bm{\tau})\in\mathscr{T}_{d}^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}
and
e\in E_{\operatorname{int}}(T)
, we obtain
(T/e,\bm{\tau}^{\prime})\in\mathscr{T}_{d}^{\ell-\ell_{e},\ell^{\prime}+\ell_{e}}
, where
T/e
is the contracted ribbon tree and the switching data
\bm{\tau}^{\prime}
is defined as follows: Suppose
e
is the edge
e^{t(e)}_{i}
, i.e., the
i
th incoming edge of
t(e)
. Let
v
be the vertex of
T/e
obtained by contracting
e
. Then

\bm{\tau}^{\prime}_{v}=(\bm{\tau}_{t(e),1},\dots,\bm{\tau}_{t(e),i-1},(\bm{\tau}_{s(e)},\bm{\tau}_{e^{t(e)}_{i}},\bm{\tau}_{t(e),i}),\bm{\tau}_{t(e),i+1},\dots,\bm{\tau}_{t(e),|t(e)|-1}).

We are mainly interested in
\mathscr{T}_{d}^{\ell}\coloneqq\mathscr{T}_{d}^{\ell,0}
. Let
\accentset{\circ}{\mathscr{T}}_{d}^{\ell}
be the subset of
\mathscr{T}_{d}^{\ell}
consisting of
(T,\bm{\tau})
such that
|E_{\operatorname{int}}(T)|=d-2
, i.e.,
T
is a
binary ribbon tree
.
To each
(T,\bm{\tau})\in\mathscr{T}^{\ell}_{d}
we assign

(2.35)

\mathfrak{X}(T,\bm{\tau})=\mathfrak{X}_{-}^{d}\times\mathfrak{X}_{0}^{|E_{\operatorname{int}}(T)|+\ell}\times{\mathfrak{X}_{+}}.

More specifically, to
e\in\{e_{1},\dots,e_{d}\}\subset E_{\operatorname{ext}}(T)
with
\ell_{e}
edge switches we associate
(Y^{e}_{0},\dots,Y^{e}_{\ell_{e}})\in\mathfrak{X}_{-}\times\mathfrak{X}_{0}^{\ell_{e}}
; to
e\in E_{\operatorname{int}}(T)
we associate
(Y^{e}_{0},\dots,Y^{e}_{\ell_{e}})\in\mathfrak{X}_{0}^{\ell_{e}+1}
; and to
e=e_{0}\in E_{\operatorname{ext}}(T)
we associate
(Y^{e}_{0},\dots,Y^{e}_{\ell_{e}})\in\mathfrak{X}_{0}^{\ell_{e}}\times\mathfrak{X}_{+}
.

Given
(T,\bm{\tau})\in\mathscr{T}^{\ell}_{d}
, a
total ordering

\bm{s}=(s_{1},\dots,s_{\ell})
assigns to each edge or vertex switch a unique number from
[\ell]
. Let
S(T,\bm{\tau})
be the set of all total orderings on
(T,\bm{\tau})
. Given
\bm{s}\in S(T,\bm{\tau})
, let
\bm{s}^{e}=(s^{e}_{0},\dots,s^{e}_{\ell_{e}-1})
be the restriction of
\bm{s}
to the edge
e
.

Let
\bm{q}_{0},\dots,\bm{q}_{d}
be
\kappa
-tuples of points in
M
, let
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern .

is a tuple

where

(1)

\bm{\Gamma}^{e_{i}}=((\theta_{s^{e_{i}}_{0}}^{e_{i}},\Gamma_{0}^{e_{i}}),(\theta^{e_{i}}_{s^{e_{i}}_{1}},\Gamma_{1}^{e_{i}},l^{e_{i}}_{1})\dots,(\theta_{s^{e_{i}}_{\ell_{e_{i}}-1}}^{e_{i}},\Gamma^{e_{i}}_{\ell_{e_{i}}-1},l^{e_{i}}_{\ell_{e_{i}}-1}),(\Gamma^{e_{i}}_{\ell_{e_{i}}},l^{e_{i}}_{\ell_{e_{i}}}))

\displaystyle\Gamma_{j}^{e_{i}}\colon[0,l^{e_{i}}_{j}]\to\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q}^{i-1},\bm{q}^{i}),

for each

, with

for

; we impose the switching conditions dictated by
\bm{\tau}_{e_{0}}
;

\bm{\Gamma}^{e}=((\theta_{s^{e}_{0}}^{e},\Gamma_{0}^{e},l_{0}^{e}),(\theta^{e}_{s^{e}_{1}},\Gamma_{1}^{e},l^{e}_{1})\dots,(\theta_{s^{e}_{\ell_{e}-1}}^{e},\Gamma^{e}_{\ell_{e}-1},l^{e}_{\ell_{e}-1}),(\Gamma^{e}_{\ell_{e}},l^{e}_{\ell_{e}}))
for

with

, where

(4)

(\Gamma^{e}_{j})^{\prime}(s)=(\bm{X}+Y^{e}(l^{e}_{j},s))_{\Gamma^{e}_{j}(s)}

\Gamma^{e_{i}}_{0}(-\infty)=\bm{\gamma}_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,d
and
\Gamma^{e_{0}}_{\ell_{e_{0}}}(+\infty)=\bm{\gamma}_{0}
;

(6)

(Weights) We assign a weight
w_{e}
to each
e\in E(T)
: set
w_{e_{i}}=1
for
i=1,\dots,d
, and for each
v\in V_{\operatorname{int}}(T)
with incoming edges
e^{v}_{1},\dots,e^{v}_{|v|-1}
and outgoing edge
e^{v}_{0}
we inductively define

w_{e_{0}^{v}}=\operatorname{min}(l_{e_{0}^{v}},1)+(1-\operatorname{min}(l_{e_{0}^{v}},1))(w_{e^{v}_{1}}+\dots+w_{e^{v}_{|v|-1}}),

where
l_{e}
is the total length of
e
, i.e., the sum of all the
l^{e}_{i}
;

(7)

(Concatenation at interior vertices) for each
v\in V_{\operatorname{int}}(T)
we have

(2.36)

(\bm{\theta},\Gamma^{e_{0}^{v}}_{0}(0))=c^{{\bm{w}}_{v}}\left(\bm{\theta},\Gamma^{e^{v}_{|v|-1}}_{\ell_{e^{v}_{|v|-1}}}(l^{e^{v}_{|v|-1}}_{\ell_{e_{|v|-1}^{v}}}),\dots,\Gamma^{e^{v}_{1}}_{\ell_{e^{v}_{1}}}(l^{e^{v}_{1}}_{\ell_{e_{1}^{v}}})\right),

where
\bm{w}_{v}=(w_{e^{v}_{1}},\dots,w_{e^{v}_{|v|-1}})
and

is the weighted concatenation map with respect to
\bm{w}_{v}
.

\bm{\Gamma}_{T}\in\mathscr{M}_{T}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern o_{\bm{\gamma}_{1}}\cong|\mathfrak{T}_{d}|\otimes o_{\bm{\gamma}_{0}}[(n-2)\ell].

Hence to each element
\bm{\Gamma}_{T}\in\mathscr{M}_{T}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\bm{\gamma}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\bm{\gamma}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\bm{\gamma}\hfil$\crcr}}},\bm{\gamma}_{0};\bm{Y},\bm{\tau},\bm{s})
with
\operatorname{ind}(\bm{\Gamma}_{T})=0
there is an induced natural map

(2.38)

\mu^{d}_{\bm{\Gamma}_{T}}\colon o_{\bm{\gamma}_{d}}\otimes\dots\otimes o_{\bm{\gamma}_{1}}\to o_{\bm{\gamma}_{0}}[(n-2)\ell].

Proof.

(a) follows from Theorems
B.7
and
C.9
.

(b) Here we only sketch the argument. Let
\mathfrak{T}_{d}^{\ell}
be the space of
thickened
metric ribbon trees with
\ell
ordered marked points on them and an assignment of a
2
-element subset of
[\kappa]
to each marked point; see
[
FO97
]
for more details on the thickening of a metric ribbon tree, and also Figure
2.6
for an example of such tree. We denote by
\mathfrak{T}_{d}^{\ell}(T,\bm{\tau},\bm{s})\subset\mathfrak{T}_{d}^{\ell}
the subspace of marked thickened metric ribbon trees whose underlying combinatorial type is that of a tree
(T,\bm{\tau},\bm{s})
with
(T,\bm{\tau})\in\mathscr{T}^{\ell}_{d}
and
\bm{s}\in S(T,\bm{\tau})
, i.e., the order of marked points agrees with
\bm{s}
and the marked points are enhanced with additional data of
2
-element subsets of
[\kappa]
dictated by
\bm{\tau}
.

The manifold
\mathscr{M}_{T}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\bm{\gamma}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\bm{\gamma}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\bm{\gamma}\hfil$\crcr}}},\bm{\gamma}_{0};\bm{Y},\bm{\tau},\bm{s})
can be presented as the fiber product of

\mathfrak{T}_{d}^{\ell}(T,\bm{\tau},\bm{s})\times W^{u}(\bm{\gamma}_{d})\times\dots\times W^{u}(\bm{\gamma}_{1})\quad\mbox{ and }\quad W^{s}(\bm{\gamma}_{0})\times\displaystyle\prod_{s\in\bm{s},\tau_{s}\in\bm{\tau}}\Delta^{\tau_{s}}_{I_{s}}

by the maps

\Psi\colon W^{s}(\bm{\gamma}_{0})\times\displaystyle\prod_{s\in\bm{s},\tau_{s}\in\bm{\tau}}\Delta^{\tau_{s}}_{I_{s}}\hookrightarrow\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q})^{\times(\ell+1)},

\Phi\colon\mathfrak{T}_{d}^{\ell}(T,\bm{\tau},\bm{s})\times W^{u}(\bm{\gamma}_{d})\times\dots\times W^{u}(\bm{\gamma}_{1})\to\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q})^{\times(\ell+1)},

where
\Psi
is the natural embedding;
\Phi
is a complicated map involving various evaluation maps, switching maps and flows along vector fields from
\bm{Y}
dictated by a point of
\mathfrak{T}_{d}^{\ell}(T,\bm{\tau},\bm{s})
; and we are assuming that all the
\bm{q}^{i}=\bm{q}
. We note that the data of the
\theta^{e}_{i}
- and
l^{e}_{j}
-coordinates allows us to recover an element of
\mathfrak{T}_{d}^{\ell}(T,\bm{\tau},\bm{s})
. We refer the reader to
[
Abo11
, Section 7]
, where a similar map is explained in the context of gradient Morse flow trees inside a finite-dimensional manifold.
Such a presentation evidently provides a natural isomorphism

(2.39)

|\mathscr{M}_{T}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern o_{\bm{\gamma}_{1}}\cong|\mathfrak{T}_{d}^{\ell}|\otimes\zeta^{-n\ell}\otimes o_{\gamma_{0}},

where use coorientations of
\Delta_{I_{s}}^{\tau_{s}}
induced by orientation of
M
as in
[
KY23
, Proposition 3.4]
. We clearly have an isomorphism
|\mathfrak{T}^{\ell}_{d}|\cong|\mathfrak{T}_{d}|\otimes\zeta^{2\ell}
, induced by choosing local orientations at marked points via orientation of the page. Hence we obtain (
2.37
).
∎

.

.

. For

a tuple of critical points and generators
[\bm{\gamma}_{1}]\in o_{\bm{\gamma}_{1}},\ldots,[\bm{\gamma}_{d}]\in o_{\bm{\gamma}_{d}}
we set:

(2.40)

\displaystyle\mu^{d}_{M}\colon CM_{-*}(\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q}))\otimes\dots\otimes CM_{-*}(\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q}))\to CM_{-*}(\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q})),

\displaystyle[\bm{\gamma}_{d}]\otimes\dots\otimes[\bm{\gamma}_{1}]\mapsto\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\geq 0,\,T\in\accentset{\circ}{\mathscr{T}}_{d}^{\ell},\bm{s}\in S(T),\,\bm{\gamma}_{0}\in\mathscr{P},\\

where

(2.41)

\dagger_{d}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}j\cdot|\gamma_{j}|.

Note that for the tuple of inputs
([\bm{\gamma}_{d}]\hbar^{i_{d}},\ldots,[\bm{\gamma}_{1}]\hbar^{i_{1}})
, the sign
\dagger_{d}
is computed using the corresponding gradings of these generators, i.e.,

\dagger_{d}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}j\cdot(|\bm{\gamma}_{j}|+(2-n)i_{j}).

The operation
\mu^{d}_{M}
is then extended to tuples of generators
([\bm{\gamma}_{d}]\hbar^{j_{d}},\ldots,[\bm{\gamma}_{1}]\hbar^{j_{1}})
for all
j_{1},\ldots,j_{d}\in\mathbb{Z}
, in the same way as for the wrapped HDHF Fukaya category and we refer the reader to the discussion following (
3.12
).

Note that
\mu^{1}_{M}
coincides with the differential (
2.30
).

Theorem 2.22
.

There exists a universal data
\bm{Y}
such that the collection
\{\mu^{d}_{M},\,d\geq 1\}
defines the structure of an
A_{\infty}
-algebra on
CM_{-*}(\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q}))
, i.e., the following equations are satisfied

(2.42)

\sum_{k,d^{\prime}}(-1)^{\text{✠}_{k}}\,\mu_{M}^{d-k+1}(\bm{\gamma}_{d},\ldots,\bm{\gamma}_{k+d^{\prime}+1},\mu_{M}^{d^{\prime}}(\bm{\gamma}_{k+d^{\prime}},\ldots,\bm{\gamma}_{k+1}),\bm{\gamma}_{k},\ldots,\bm{\gamma}_{1})=0,

where

(2.43)

\text{✠}_{k}=|\bm{\gamma}_{1}|+\dots+|\bm{\gamma}_{k}|-k.

Proof.

The universal data
\bm{Y}
is given by Theorem
C.9
. The terms in the sum (
2.40
) are integers as the data
\bm{Y}
that we choose is
symmetric
, i.e., does not depend on the choice of ordering
\bm{s}\in S(T,\bm{\tau})
; see (
C.27
).

Verifying the
A_{\infty}
-relations boils down to the analysis of
\partial\mathscr{M}_{T}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\bm{\gamma}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\bm{\gamma}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\bm{\gamma}\hfil$\crcr}}},\bm{\gamma}_{0};\bm{Y},\bm{\tau},\bm{s})
for
\operatorname{ind}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\bm{\gamma}\hfil$\crcr}}},\bm{\gamma}_{0},T)=1
, as given by Theorem
C.9
. The contributions corresponding to (
C.9
) with
i>0
cancel out as in Lemma
2.10
and Corollary
C.7
. The contributions corresponding to (
C.9
) consist of interior points and hence cancel out, as in the proof of Theorem
C.9
. The local behavior is illustrated in Figure
2.7
.

Next we analyze the contributions from edge contractions (
C.9
). Such a boundary point
\bm{\Gamma}_{T}
is an element of
\mathscr{M}_{T^{\prime}}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\bm{\gamma}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\bm{\gamma}\hfil$\crcr}}},\bm{\gamma}_{0};\bm{Y},\bm{\tau}^{\prime},\bm{s})
with
(T^{\prime}=T/e,\bm{\tau}^{\prime})
. Since
T
is binary, there is a unique binary tree
\tilde{T}\not=T
and a unique edge
\tilde{e}\in\tilde{T}
such that
\tilde{T}/\tilde{e}=T^{\prime}
. Hence
\bm{\Gamma}_{T}\in\partial\mathscr{M}_{\tilde{T}}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern for some
\tilde{\bm{\tau}}
and the contributions from (

) also cancel out. See
[
