, Lemma 5.19, Corollary 5.20]
for more details in the finite-dimensional case.

The remaining boundary points (
C.31
) correspond precisely to the compositions
\mu_{M}^{d-d^{\prime}}\circ\mu_{M}^{d^{\prime}}
, implying the theorem. The signs induced by the boundary orientation and by the product orientation agree up to an overall sign dictated by (
2.42
); this is verified in the same way as in Section
3.1
for the Floer-theoretic moduli spaces. The main observation here is that the space
\mathfrak{T}_{d}
is diffeomorphic to the space of
(d+1)
-punctured disks
\mathscr{R}^{d}
(see the discussion below)
[
FO97a
]
, and the orientation conventions for
\mathfrak{T}_{d}
(see
[
Abo11
, Section 8]
) coincide with those for
\mathscr{R}^{d}
.
∎

Figure 2.7
.
The crosses denote switchings.

3.
The isomorphism with HDHF

In this section we define an
A_{\infty}
-morphism from the wrapped HDHF
A_{\infty}
-algebra of
\kappa
cotangent fibers of
T^{*}M
to the based multiloop complex

\mathscr{F}=(\mathscr{F}^{d}\colon CW^{*}(\sqcup_{i=1}^{\kappa}T_{q_{i}}^{*}M)\to CM^{*+1-d}(\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q})))_{d\geq 1},

by counting rigid elements in a mixed moduli space which combines holomorphic curves and Morse gradient trees and
show that
\mathscr{F}
is an
A_{\infty}
-equivalence.

3.1.
Review of HDHF

We briefly sketch the construction of
CW^{*}(\sqcup_{i=1}^{\kappa}T_{q_{i}}^{*}M)
, referring the reader to
[
HTY a
, Sections 2.1 and 2.2]
for more details.

Let
X=T^{*}M
be the cotangent bundle of
M
with canonical Liouville form
\lambda=p\,dq
. As before, let
g
be a Riemannian metric on
M
and
|\cdot|
be the induced norm on
T^{*}M
. Choose the Hamiltonian

(3.1)

\displaystyle H\colon T^{*}M\to\mathbb{R},\quad(q,p)\mapsto\tfrac{1}{2}|p|^{2},

where
q\in M
and
p\in T^{*}_{q}M
. The Hamiltonian vector field
X_{H}
with respect to the canonical symplectic form
\omega=dq\wedge dp
is then given by
i_{X_{H}}\omega=dH
. Let
\phi^{t}\coloneqq\phi^{t}_{H}
be the time-
t
flow of
X_{H}
.

The objects of the wrapped HDHF Fukaya category that we consider are
ordered

\kappa
-tuples of disjoint exact Lagrangians in
(X,\omega=d\lambda)
that are cylindrical at infinity. In particular we consider
\sqcup_{i=1}^{\kappa}T_{q_{i}}^{*}M
, where
\bm{q}=(q_{1},\dots,q_{\kappa})
is an ordered
\kappa
-tuple of distinct points on a small disk
U\subset{M}
.
By choosing
g
, we can guarantee that all Hamiltonian chords of
\phi^{1}
between the cotangent fibers
\{T_{q_{1}}^{*}{M},\dots,T_{q_{\kappa}}^{*}{M}\}
are nondegenerate. We assume that
c_{1}(T^{*}M)=0
and all cotangent fibers are relatively spin. Then there exists a well-defined
\mathbb{Z}
-grading on the Hamiltonian chords
y
connecting
T_{q_{i}}^{*}M
and
T_{q_{j}}^{*}M
for any such pair and to each
y
one may associate a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded rank
1
free abelian group
o_{y}
called
orientation line
, whose
\mathbb{Z}
-grading is equal to that of the chord
y
. As a graded
\mathbb{Z}
-module the chain complex
CW^{*}(T^{*}_{q_{i}}M,T^{*}_{q_{j}}M)
is a direct sum of such orientation lines over all Hamiltonian chords
y
from
T^{*}_{q_{i}}M
to
T^{*}_{q_{j}}M
. See
[
Sei08
,
Abo10
,
Gan13
]
for more details.

We set

CW^{*}_{0}(\sqcup_{i=1}^{\kappa}T_{q_{i}}^{*}M)=\bigoplus_{\sigma\in S_{\kappa}}\otimes_{i=1}^{\kappa}CW^{*}(T_{q_{i}}^{*}M,T_{q_{\sigma(i)}}^{*}M)

To each tuple of chords
\bm{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{\kappa})
we associate an orientation line

o_{\bm{y}}=o_{y_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes o_{y_{\kappa}}

with
\mathbb{Z}
-grading given by

|\bm{y}|=|y_{1}|+\dots+|y_{\kappa}|.

Such orientation lines are the direct summands of
CW^{*}_{0}(\sqcup_{i=1}^{\kappa}T_{q_{i}}^{*}M)
.

We then define the (module underlying the)
wrapped HDHF chain complex
CW^{*}(\sqcup_{i=1}^{\kappa}T_{q_{i}}^{*}M)
to be
CW^{*}_{0}(\sqcup_{i=1}^{\kappa}T_{q_{i}}^{*}M)\otimes_{R}R\llbracket\hbar\rrbracket
for
n=2
and
CW^{*}_{0}(\sqcup_{i=1}^{\kappa}T_{q_{i}}^{*}M)\otimes_{R}R[\hbar]
for
n>2
.

Let
\mathscr{R}^{d}
be the moduli space of unit disks
S
with
d+1
boundary punctures, where
d
of them are marked as incoming and
1
as outgoing, modulo automorphisms. The punctures of
S
are
p_{0},\dots,p_{d}
, arranged in a counterclockwise manner, where
p_{0}
is the unique outgoing puncture. Let
\partial_{i}S
be the boundary arc from
p_{i}
to
p_{i+1}
. We choose representatives
S
of equivalence classes of
\mathscr{R}^{d}
in a smooth manner (e.g., by setting
p_{0}=-i
and
p_{1}=i
) and abuse notation by writing
S\in\mathscr{R}^{d}
.
We call
S
the “
A_{\infty}
-base direction”.
We endow each
S
with a collection of strip-like ends
[0,+\infty)_{s_{i}}\times[0,1]_{t_{i}}
about
p_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,d
, and
(-\infty,0]_{s_{0}}\times[0,1]_{t_{0}}
about
p_{0}
, and we make such a choice in a universal and consistent manner as explained in
[
Sei08
, Section (9g)]
.

We denote by
\mathscr{R}^{d}_{\kappa,\chi}
the moduli space of simple (= with simple branch points)
\kappa
-fold branched covers
\pi:\dot{F}\to S
, where
\dot{F}=F\setminus\cup_{i=0}^{d}{\bf p_{i}}
is a compact Riemann surface with boundary and boundary punctures and each
\bf p_{i}
is the
\kappa
-tuple of points in
\partial F
mapping to the
i
th boundary puncture of
S
under the extension of
\pi
to
F\to S
. Here
S
ranges over all of
\mathscr{R}^{d}
,
\chi(\dot{F})=\chi
, and all the branch points are assumed to lie in
\operatorname{int}(S)
. Note that for each such
(\dot{F},\pi)\in\mathscr{R}^{d}_{\kappa,\chi}
there are exactly
\kappa-\chi
branch points.

We make a universal choice of conformally consistent Floer data
(J^{d,\kappa,\chi},H^{d,\kappa,\chi})
which assigns to each
(\dot{F},\pi)\in\mathscr{R}^{d}_{\kappa,\chi}
a family of almost complex structures
J^{d,\kappa,\chi}_{\dot{F},\pi}\colon\dot{F}\to\mathscr{J}(T^{*}M)
and a family of Hamiltonian functions
H^{d,\kappa,\chi}_{\dot{F},\pi}\colon\dot{F}\to\mathscr{H}(T^{*}M)
. Here
\mathscr{J}(T^{*}M)
is the set of compatible almost complex structures of contact type and
\mathscr{H}(T^{*}M)
is the set of quadratic-at-infinity Hamiltonian functions. We refer to
[
HTY a
,
KY23
,
Sei08
]
for more details on constructing such consistent data.

Referring to
[
Abo10
]
for more details, we choose a function
\rho_{S}\colon\partial\overline{S}\to[1,+\infty)
and a
1
-form
\alpha_{S}
on each
S\in\mathscr{R}^{d}
such that
\alpha_{S}|_{\partial D}=0
,
d\alpha_{S}\leq 0
, and for the choice as above it is required that

(3.2)

\displaystyle X_{H^{d,\kappa,\chi}_{\dot{F},\pi}}\otimes\pi^{*}(\alpha_{S})=X_{\frac{H}{\rho_{S}(p_{i})}\circ\psi^{\rho_{S}(p_{i})}}\otimes dt,

(3.3)

\displaystyle J^{d,\kappa,\chi}_{\dot{F},\pi}=(\psi^{\rho_{S}(p_{i})})^{*}J^{d,\kappa,\chi}_{\dot{F},\pi}=J_{t}

on each strip-like end, where
J_{t}
,
t\in[0,1]
, is a fixed path in
\mathscr{J}(T^{*}M)
.

Given
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\bm{y}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\bm{y}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern and
\bm{y}_{0}
, where each
\bm{y}_{i}\in CW^{*}(\sqcup_{j=1}^{\kappa}T^{*}_{q_{j}}M)
, we denote by
\mathscr{M}_{\kappa,\chi}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\bm{y}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\bm{y}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\bm{y}\hfil$\crcr}}};\bm{y}_{0})
the moduli space of maps

(3.4)

u=(\pi,v)\colon(\dot{F},j)\to(S\times T^{*}M,J_{S}),

where
\pi:(\dot{F},j)\to S
represents a point in
\mathscr{R}^{d}_{\kappa,\chi}
and
\pi_{T^{*}M}\circ u=v
satisfies:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{$(dv-X_{H^{d,\kappa,\chi}_{\dot{F},\pi}}\otimes\pi^{*}(\alpha_{S}))^{0,1}=0$;}\\
\text{$v(z)\in\sqcup_{i}T^{*}_{q_{i}}M$ for each $z\in\partial F$;}\\
\text{$v$ limits to $\psi^{\rho_{S}(p_{i})}(\bm{y}_{i})$ as $s_{i}\to+\infty$ for $i=1,\dots,d$;}\\
\text{$v$ limits to $\psi^{\rho_{S}(p_{0})}(\bm{y}_{0})$ as $s_{0}\to-\infty$.}\end{array}\right.

The Fredholm index of a curve
u\in\mathscr{M}_{\kappa,\chi}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern is given by the formula

(3.9)

\operatorname{ind}(u)=(n-2)(\chi-\kappa)+|\bm{y}_{0}|-|\bm{y}_{1}|-\dots-|\bm{y}_{d}|+d-2.

Note that above we used the isomorphism
|\mathscr{R}^{d}_{\kappa,\chi}|\cong|\mathscr{R}^{d}|\otimes\zeta^{2(\kappa-\chi)}
, which relies on the following. Let
\mathscr{R}^{d,\kappa-\chi}
be the moduli space of disks
S
with
\kappa-\chi
interior marked points, for example studied in
[
Sie21
, Section 4]
to define
bulk deformations
. There is a covering map
\mathscr{R}^{d}_{\kappa,\chi}\to\mathscr{R}^{d,\kappa-\chi}
and a fibration
\mathscr{R}^{d,\kappa-\chi}\to\mathscr{R}^{d}
. We then choose the standard orientation conventions for
\mathscr{R}^{d}
as in
[
Sei08
]
and orient the fibers using the orientation on
S
at each marked point (note that the marked points are ordered). This orientation on
\mathscr{R}^{d,\kappa-\chi}
clearly induces an orientation
\mathscr{R}^{d}_{\kappa,\chi}
.

Now we explain how to extend
\partial_{u}
to a map

(3.12)

\partial_{u}\colon o_{\bm{y}_{d}}[(n-2)j_{d}]\otimes\dots\otimes o_{\bm{y}_{1}}[(n-2)j_{1}]\to o_{\bm{y}_{0}}[(n-2)(j_{1}+\dots+j_{d}+(\kappa-\chi))].

For simplicity suppose only one

. Then (
3.10
) induces the isomorphism

we then get
\partial_{u}
in (
3.12
).
The
\mu^{d}
-composition map is then defined as

(3.22)

\mu^{d}_{F}([\bm{y}_{d}]\hbar^{j_{d}},\dots,[\bm{y}_{1}]\hbar^{j_{1}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{y}_{0},\chi\leq\kappa\\
u\in\mathscr{M}_{\kappa,\chi}^{\operatorname{ind}=0}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern where
[\bm{y}_{d}]\hbar^{j_{d}}\in o_{\bm{y}_{d}}[(n-2)j_{d}],\dots,[\bm{y}_{1}]\hbar^{j_{1}}\in o_{\bm{y}_{1}}[(n-2)j_{1}]
and

(3.23)

\dagger_{d}=|\bm{y}_{1}|+2|\bm{y}_{2}|+\dots+d|\bm{y}_{d}|.

The proof of the
A_{\infty}
-relations (see (
2.42
)) up to signs is outlined in
[
CHT
,
HTY a
]
and here we only provide a discussion regarding signs. As pointed out in
[
CHT
]
,
\partial\overline{\mathscr{M}}^{\operatorname{ind}=1}_{\kappa,\chi}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\bm{y}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\bm{y}\hfil$\crcr}}};\bm{y}_{0})
is covered by

(3.24)

\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\bm{y}};\,\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\leq\kappa\\
\chi_{1}+\chi_{2}=\kappa+\chi\end{subarray}}\mathscr{M}^{\operatorname{ind}=0}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}(\bm{y}_{p+d_{1}},\dots,\bm{y}_{p+1};\tilde{\bm{y}})\times\mathscr{M}^{\operatorname{ind}=0}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}(\bm{y}_{d},\dots,\bm{y}_{p+d_{1}+1},\tilde{\bm{y}},\bm{y}_{p},\dots,\bm{y}_{1};\bm{y}_{0}).

It remains to analyze the difference between the boundary orientation and the chosen product orientation as in
[
Sei08
, (12f), (12g)]
. We claim that the analysis is essentially the same and follows from a commutative diagram analogous to the one presented in
[
Sei08
, (12f)]
. The key observation here is that

\operatorname{dim}\mathscr{R}^{d}=\operatorname{dim}\mathscr{R}^{d}_{\kappa,\chi}\,\operatorname{mod}\,2,

and the fact that the Koszul sign in (
3.1
) naturally incorporates into such diagram.

Remark 3.1
.

We note that it is expected that the HDHF as described above arises from the appropriate Floer theory on the orbifold
\operatorname{Sym}^{\kappa}(T^{*}M)
. We do not pursue this approach here and refer the reader to
[
MSS25
]
for the development of this perspective.

3.2.
Half-disk Hurwitz spaces

We denote by
\mathscr{H}^{d}
the moduli space of unit disks with
d+2
boundary punctures of which
d
are incoming and appear consecutively along the boundary and
2
are outgoing, modulo automorphisms. We refer to
[
Abo12
, Section 4.4]
for a detailed treatment of these spaces. We denote by
p_{-1},p_{0},\dots,p_{d}
the punctures of
T\in\mathscr{H}^{d}
appearing in counterclockwise order, where
p_{-1},p_{0}
are the outgoing punctures. The portion of the boundary between
p_{i}
and
p_{i+1}
(we assume
p_{d+1}=p_{-1}
) will be denoted
\partial_{i}T
as before and the portion between
p_{-1}
and
p_{0}
, denoted
\partial_{\operatorname{out}}T
, will be called the
outgoing segment
. Since
\partial_{\operatorname{out}}T
is a preferred segment, we will often refer to
T\in\mathscr{H}^{d}
as a “half-disk”.

Now observe that
\mathscr{H}^{d}\cong\mathscr{R}^{d+1}
. This identification provides us with a smooth choice of representatives of equivalence classes of
\mathscr{H}^{d}
and a universal choice of strip-like ends as in Section
3.1
. We also notice that
\mathscr{H}^{1}
consists of just one point
T
, corresponding to the twice-punctured half-strip
[0,+\infty)\times[0,1]
. As before we have the natural projections

\pi_{T^{*}M}:T\times{T^{*}M}\to{T^{*}M}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\pi_{T}:T\times{T^{*}M}\to T.

We further introduce the moduli space
\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\kappa,\chi}
of
\kappa
-fold simple branched covers
\pi\colon\dot{F}\to T
, with
T\in\mathscr{H}^{d}
,
\chi(\dot{F})=\chi
, and
\dot{F}=F\setminus\cup_{i=-1}^{d}\bm{p}_{i}
, where each
\bm{p}_{i}
is a
\kappa
-tuple of boundary punctures of
F
, given by the
\kappa
preimages of the puncture
p_{i}
of
T
. All the branched points are assumed to lie in
\operatorname{int}(T)
.

Again, we make a universal and conformally consistent choice of Floer data
(J^{d,\kappa,\chi},H^{d,\kappa,\chi},\rho^{d},\alpha^{d})
for all tuples
(d,\kappa,\chi)
, which assigns to each
(\dot{F},\pi)\in\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\kappa,\chi}
a family of almost complex structures
J^{d,\kappa,\chi}_{\dot{F},\pi}\colon\dot{F}\to\mathscr{J}(T^{*}M)
and a family of Hamiltonian functions
H^{d,\kappa,\chi}_{\dot{F},\pi}\colon\dot{F}\to\mathscr{H}(T^{*}M)
. Additionally, there is a choice of a function
\rho^{d}_{T}\colon\partial\overline{T}\to[1,+\infty)
and a
1
-form
\alpha^{d}_{T}
on each
T\in\mathscr{H}^{d}
such that
\alpha^{d}_{T}|_{\partial D}=0
,
d\alpha_{T}\leq 0
, and for the choice as above we require

(3.25)

\displaystyle X_{H^{d,\kappa,\chi}_{\dot{F},\pi}}\otimes\pi^{*}(\alpha_{T})=X_{\frac{H}{\rho_{T}(p_{i})}\circ\psi^{\rho_{T}(p_{i})}}\otimes dt\text{ for }1\leq i\leq d,

(3.26)

\displaystyle J^{d,\kappa,\chi}_{\dot{F},\pi}=(\psi^{\rho_{T}(p_{i})})^{*}J^{d,\kappa,\chi}_{\dot{F},\pi}=J_{t}\text{ for }-1\leq i\leq d,

on each strip-like end as above, where
J_{t}
,
t\in[0,1]
, is a fixed path in
\mathscr{J}(T^{*}M)
. In addition, we require the restrictions of
H^{d,\kappa,\chi}_{\dot{F},\pi}
to the preimages of
\partial_{\operatorname{out}}T
to vanish.
We also assume that
\rho^{d}_{T}\equiv 1
near
p_{-1}
and
p_{0}
.
We refer to
[
Abo12
, Section 4.5]
for additional details.

Given a tuple
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\bm{y}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\bm{y}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern with each

, let
\mathscr{H}_{\kappa,\chi}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern be the moduli space of holomorphic maps

\displaystyle u=(\pi,v)\colon(\dot{F},j)\to(T\times T^{*}M,J_{T})

(3.27)

\displaystyle(dv-X_{H^{d,\kappa,\chi}_{\dot{F},\pi}}\otimes\pi^{*}(\alpha_{T}))^{0,1}=0

such that
((\dot{F},j),\pi)\in\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\kappa,\chi}
and the following boundary conditions hold:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{$v(z)\in\sqcup_{j}T^{*}_{q_{j}}M$ for each $z\in\pi^{-1}(\partial_{i}T)$ for $i=0,1,\dots,d$;}\\
\text{$v(z)\in M$ for each $z\in\pi^{-1}(\partial_{\operatorname{out}}T)$;}\\
\text{$v$ limits to $\psi^{\rho_{T}(p_{i})}(\bm{y}_{i})$ as $s_{i}\to+\infty$ for $i=0,1,\dots,d$;}\\
\text{$v$ limits to $\bm{q}$ as $s_{i}\to-\infty$ for $i=-1$.}\end{array}\right.

The choice of Floer data as above guarantees that each
\mathscr{H}_{\kappa,\chi}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern is a smooth manifold of dimension

(n-2)(\kappa-\chi)-|\bm{y}_{1}|-\dots-|\bm{y}_{d}|+d-1.

This index computation is analogous to the one from

, Lemma 4.3]
.

As in (

) we have a natural isomorphism of orientation line bundles

|\mathscr{H}_{\kappa,\chi}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 3.3.
The linear map
\mathscr{F}^{1}

In this subsection we define the linear component
\mathscr{F}^{1}
of the morphism
\mathscr{F}
between the two
A_{\infty}
-algebras of interest by counting elements of
mixed moduli spaces
. Given
\bm{y}\in CW^{*}(\sqcup_{i}T_{q_{i}}^{*}M)
, we define

(3.34)

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}^{1}(\bm{y})=\sum_{{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{\gamma},\,r\geq 0\\
(u,\bm{\Gamma})\in\mathscr{P}_{r}(\bm{y},\bm{\Gamma})\end{subarray}}}(-1)^{|\bm{y}|}\mathscr{F}^{1}_{(u,\bm{\Gamma})}([\bm{y}]),

where
\bm{\gamma}\in CM_{-*}(\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q}))
,
the elements in
\mathscr{P}_{r}(\bm{y},\bm{\gamma})
are schematically given by Figure
3.1
, and

(3.35)

{\operatorname{ind}({\bf y},\bm{\gamma},r)\coloneqq-(n-2)r+|\bm{\gamma}|-|{\bf y}|}

refers to the virtual dimension of
\mathscr{P}_{r}(\bm{y},\bm{\gamma})
.

Figure 3.1
.
A schematic description of an element in
\mathscr{P}(\bm{y},\bm{\gamma})
. The upper half denotes the base direction of an HDHF holomorphic branched cover of a half-disk in
T^{*}M
, where the bottom edge lies on the zero section
M
, viewed as an element
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}\in\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q})
; the lower arc is an MFLS from
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
to
\bm{\gamma}
; the sum of the number of branch points on the holomorphic curve (or more precisely the branching number) and the number of switching points of the MFLS is
r

.

3.3.1.
Mixed moduli spaces
\mathscr{P}_{r}(\bm{y},\bm{\gamma})

Each element of
\mathscr{P}_{r}(\bm{y},\bm{\gamma})
is a tuple
((\dot{F},\pi),u),\bm{\Gamma},\bm{s})
(we also write
(u,\bm{\Gamma})
if
\bm{s}
is understood or unimportant), where:

(A) The tuple
\bm{s}
is a total ordering of
[r]
. The curve
((\dot{F},\pi),u)
is an element of the space
\mathscr{H}_{\kappa,\chi}(\bm{y})
for some
\kappa,\chi\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
b=\kappa-\chi\geq 0
. In particular,
\pi\colon\dot{F}\to T
is a branched cover of the half-strip
T=[0,+\infty)\times[0,1]\setminus\{p_{-1}=(0,1),p_{0}=(0,0)\}
, where
p_{1}=+\infty
. We require that the restriction of
\bm{s}
to the first
b
elements agrees with the ordering of the branch points of
u
. Then
(\theta_{s_{1}},\dots,\theta_{s_{b}})
are the
[0,1]
-coordinates of the corresponding branch points of
(\dot{F},\pi)\in\mathscr{H}^{1}_{\kappa,\chi}
.

(B) Before defining
\bm{\Gamma}
we introduce the evaluation map

(3.36)

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}:\mathscr{H}_{\kappa,\chi}(\bm{y})\to\Omega^{1,2}(M,{\bm{q}}),

\displaystyle(u,\bm{\Gamma})\mapsto\mathscr{E}(u),

as follows: If
u
does not have any branch points along
\partial_{\operatorname{out}}T
, then we let
\mathscr{E}(u)
be the restriction of
u
to
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}=\pi^{-1}(\partial_{\operatorname{out}}T)
, precomposed with
\nu_{[r]}^{\circ b}
evaluated at
(\theta_{s_{1}},\dots,\theta_{s_{r}},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime})
, where the notation
f^{\circ k}
means the composition of
f
with itself
k
times.
If
u
has branch points along
\partial_{\operatorname{out}}T
, then let

\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}=\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}v|_{\pi^{-1}(\{\delta\}\times[0,1])},

and we set

(3.37)

\mathscr{E}(u)=(\gamma^{\prime}_{1}\circ\nu_{[r]}^{\circ b},\dots,\gamma^{\prime}_{\kappa}\circ\nu_{[r]}^{\circ b}).

Observe that
\mathscr{E}(u)\in\Omega^{m,2}(M,\bm{q})
for arbitrary
m\geq 1
.

(C)
\bm{\Gamma}
is an MFLS which “starts” at
\mathscr{E}(u)
(below we set
\ell=r-b
)

\bm{\Gamma}=\left((\theta_{s_{b+1}},\Gamma_{0},l_{0}),(\theta_{s_{b+2}},\Gamma_{1},l_{1}),\dots,(\theta_{s_{r}},\Gamma_{\ell-1},l_{\ell-1}),\Gamma_{\ell}\right),

where
\Gamma_{0}\colon[0,l_{0}]\to\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q})
is a continuously differentiable map satisfying
\Gamma_{0}(0)=\mathscr{E}(u)
;
\Gamma_{\ell}(+\infty)=\bm{\gamma}
; and the remaining conditions are similar to the ones in Definition
2.8
and we omit the details.

Note that
\mathscr{P}_{r}(\bm{y},\bm{\gamma})
depends on the choice of Floer data
(J^{1,\kappa,\chi},H^{1,\kappa,\chi})
and perturbation data
\bm{Y}^{\ell}
. It admits a natural decomposition
\mathscr{P}_{r}(\bm{y},\bm{\gamma})=\bigsqcup_{b+\ell=r}\mathscr{P}_{b,\ell}(\bm{y},\bm{\gamma})
, where
b
is the number of branch points and
\ell
is the number of switches.

Lemma 3.2
.

There exists a universal Floer data
(J^{1,\kappa,\chi},H^{1,\kappa,\chi})
and a universal perturbation data
\bm{Y}^{\ell}
for all tuples
(\kappa,\chi,\ell)
such that all spaces
\mathscr{P}_{r}(\bm{y},\bm{\gamma})
are smooth manifolds of dimension
\operatorname{ind}({\bf y},\bm{\gamma},\ell)
.
Moreover, if
\operatorname{ind}({\bf y},\bm{\gamma},\ell)\leq 1
, then
\mathscr{P}_{r}(\bm{y},\bm{\gamma})
admits a compactification.

Proof.

To show that
\mathscr{P}_{b,\ell}(\bm{y},\bm{\gamma})
is a smooth manifold we use the fact that there is a choice of universal Floer data for which the maps

\mathscr{E}\colon\mathscr{H}_{\kappa,\chi}(\bm{y})\to\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q})

are immersions. We refer the reader to
[
AS10
, Section 5]
and
[
MS12
, Section 3.4]
for additional details. We can then show that for a generic choice of
\bm{Y}^{\ell}
the moduli space
\mathscr{P}_{b,\ell}(\bm{y},\bm{\gamma})
is smooth by applying Lemma
B.5
as in the proof of Theorem
B.6
. Here we omit the precise definition of the universality of the perturbation data
\bm{Y}^{\ell}
and simply refer to the analogous discussion in Appendix
C
.
The universality of the Floer data ensures the existence of a compactification and we delay a more careful discussion of the boundary strata until the next section.
∎

Given an element
(\bm{u},\bm{\Gamma})\in\mathscr{P}_{r}(\bm{y},\bm{\gamma})
we have a canonical isomorphism

(3.38)

|\mathscr{P}_{r}(\bm{y},\bm{\gamma})|\cong o_{\bm{\gamma}}\otimes|\mathscr{H}_{\kappa,\chi}(\bm{y})|

Whenever the dimension of
\mathscr{P}_{r}(\bm{y},\bm{\gamma})
the above together with (
3.33
) provides a natural map

(3.39)

\mathscr{F}^{1}_{(\bm{u},\bm{\Gamma})}\colon o_{\bm{y}}\to o_{\bm{\gamma}}[(n-2)r],

that we use to define the chain map (
3.34
).

3.3.2.
Ghost bubbles and Kuranishi replacements

As discussed in Section 3.7 and Lemma 3.9.3 of
[
CHT
]
, a technical difficulty that we face is the formation of ghost bubbles. More precisely, a sequence of curves
(u_{i},\bm{\Gamma}_{(i)})
,
i=1,2,\dots
, in
\mathscr{P}_{r}(\bm{y},\bm{\gamma})
, after passing to a subsequence, can limit to

(u_{\infty}=u_{\infty,1}\cup u_{\infty,2},\bm{\Gamma}_{(\infty)}),

where:

(i)

the
main part

u_{\infty,1}
is the union of components that are not ghost bubbles; each component of the
u_{\infty,1}
is somewhere injective; and

(ii)

u_{\infty,2}
is a union of
ghost bubbles
, i.e., locally constant maps where the domain is a possibly disconnected compact Riemann surface (possibly with boundary) and
u_{\infty,2}
maps to points on
\operatorname{Im}(u_{\infty,1})
.

Let
F
be a connected compact oriented surface with boundary and let
\mathscr{M}^{F}
be the moduli space of holomorphic maps
u:(F,j)\to T\times T^{*}M
that are constant and such that
u
maps
\partial F
to
\partial_{\operatorname{out}}T
times the zero section
M
. Each component of
u_{\infty,2}
belongs to some
\mathscr{M}^{F}
.

When
n=2
, we can eliminate ghost bubbles using Ekholm-Shende
[
ES22
, Theorem 1.1]
(which in turn builds on work of Doan-Walpuski
[
DW19
]
) as in
[
CHT
, Lemma 3.9.3]
. Note that the Ekholm-Shende result also applies to the case of boundary nodes along Lagrangians.

On the other hand, when
n\geq 3
, we excise the portion
\mathscr{B}
of
\mathscr{P}_{r}(\bm{y},\bm{\gamma})
that is close to breaking into
u_{\infty}=u_{\infty,1}\cup u_{\infty,2}
with
u_{\infty,2}\not=\varnothing
and replace it with a Kuranishi model
\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
of a neighborhood of all possible
u_{\infty,1}\cup u_{\infty,2}
satisfying (i) and (ii).
This gives us the
Kuranishi replacements

\mathscr{P}^{\sharp}_{r}(\bm{y},\bm{\gamma}),\quad\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}_{\kappa,\chi}(\bm{y},\bm{y}^{\prime}),\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathscr{M}^{F,\sharp}

of
\mathscr{P}_{r}(\bm{y},\bm{\gamma})
,
\mathscr{M}_{\kappa,\chi}(\bm{y},\bm{y}^{\prime})
, and
\mathscr{M}^{F}
for all
\chi
,
{\bf y}
,
{\bf y}^{\prime}
,
\bm{\gamma}
, and
F
such that:

(KR1)

\mathscr{P}^{\sharp}_{r}(\bm{y},\bm{\gamma})
and
\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}_{\kappa,\chi}(\bm{y},\bm{y}^{\prime})
are transversely cut out branched manifolds;

(KR2)

\mathscr{M}^{F,\sharp}
is a transversely cut out branched manifold which consists of perturbed holomorphic maps from connected compact Riemann surfaces
(F,j)
which are homologous to a constant map; and

(KR3)

all the strata of
\partial\mathscr{P}^{\sharp}_{r}(\bm{y},\bm{\gamma})
are finite fiber products of moduli spaces of the form
\mathscr{P}^{\sharp}_{r^{\prime}}(\bm{y}^{\prime},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime})
,
\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}_{\kappa,\chi^{\prime}}(\bm{y}^{\prime},\bm{y}^{\prime\prime})
, and
\mathscr{M}^{F,\sharp}
.

The Kuranishi replacement can be constructed using the work of Fukaya-Oh-Ohta-Ono
[
FOOO09
,
FOOO09a
,
FOOO20
]
or its variant due to McDuff-Wehrheim
[
MW17
,
McD19
]
. (Also see virtual fundamental cycle techniques of Hofer-Wysocki-Zehnder
[
HWZ17
]
and Pardon
[
Par16
]
.)

In what follows we assume that for
n\geq 3
we pass to the relevant Kuranishi replacement as necessary and omit
\sharp
from the notation. Kuranishi replacements will also be made without mention for analogous moduli spaces. Note that the use of Kuranishi replacements forces us to use
\mathbb{Q}
-coefficients; see Remark
1.1
.

3.3.3.
Chain map

Theorem 3.3
.

\mathscr{F}^{1}
is a chain map.

Sketch of proof.

We analyze the codimension
1
boundary of

\mathscr{P}_{r}\coloneqq\mathscr{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{ind}=1}(\bm{y},\bm{\gamma}).

A new codimension
1
phenomenon is the nodal degeneration along the zero section
M
, that is, some branch point or switch point may approach the bottom boundary of the
A_{\infty}
-base disk. We give a sketch of the proof that this degeneration admits a continuation inside
\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{r}
.

Step 1 (Elimination of some nodal degenerations)

Consider
(u_{*},\bm{\Gamma})\in\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{r}
, where
u_{*}\in\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{\kappa,\chi}(\bm{y})
is a nodal curve with
k
boundary nodes along
\pi^{-1}(\partial_{\operatorname{out}}T)
in addition to
b=\kappa-\chi
branch points (here the nodes are counted with multiplicity).
The projection of each node to
T
is assigned a
[0,1]
-coordinate; we then assign a
k
-tuple
\bm{\theta}_{k}
to the
k
boundary nodes and choose some ordering on it. Then we may evaluate
\mathscr{E}(u_{*})
as in (
3.37
), after complementing with an additional
(r-b-k)
-tuple of
\theta
-coordinates. Observe that
\mathscr{E}(u_{*})
belongs to a deeper stratum where pairs of strands intersect at each of the
\theta
-coordinates in
\bm{\theta}_{k}
. We then denote by
sw(\mathscr{E}(u_{*}))
the multiloop obtained by applying
k
corresponding switching maps in the order previously chosen.

Figure
3.2
gives a schematic description of a neighborhood of
\mathscr{E}(u_{*})\in\Omega^{1,2}(M,{\bm{q}})
when
k=1
; in the figure
\mathscr{E}(u_{*})
is identified with
sw(\mathscr{E}(u_{*}))
.

Figure 3.2
.
The neighborhood of
\mathscr{E}(u_{*})
in
\Omega^{1,2}(M,{\bm{q}})
, where
u_{*}
is a nodal curve and
\mathscr{E}(u_{*})
is identified with
sw(\mathscr{E}(u_{*}))
. We are considering the
n=2
case. The
1
-dimensional manifolds
A
and
B
are given by thick black and red lines,
A^{\prime}
is one-half of
A
, and the intersection of
A
and
B
represents
sw(\mathscr{E}(u_{*}))\equiv\mathscr{E}(u_{*})
.
The blue arcs denote MFLS: the pair (I,II) denotes the MFLS from
\mathscr{E}(u_{1})
to
\bm{\gamma}
whilst the blue arc III denotes the MFLS from
\mathscr{E}(u_{2})
to
\bm{\gamma}
. The dotted arc
C
indicates where switchings along gradient trajectories take place. The tuples
(u_{1},(\mathrm{I,II}))
and
(u_{2},\mathrm{III})
are viewed as elements of
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{ind}=1}(\bm{y},\bm{\gamma})
.
Here
\mathscr{E}(u_{2})
approaches
\mathscr{E}(u_{*})
and then continues to
\mathscr{E}(u_{1})
.

Let

(i)

B
be the set of
\mathscr{E}(u_{2})
,
u_{2}\in\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{\kappa,\chi-k}(\bm{y})
(i.e.,
u_{2}
has
b+k
branch points), such that there is an MFLS
\bm{\Gamma}_{2}
from
\mathscr{E}(u_{2})
to
\bm{\gamma}
with
\ell=r-b-k
switches as in (C) in the definition of
\mathscr{P}_{r}
;

(ii)

A
be the set of
\mathscr{E}(u_{1})
,
u_{1}\in\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{\kappa,\chi}(\bm{y})
; and

(iii)

A^{\prime}\subset A
be the subset consisting of
\mathscr{E}(u_{1})
,
u_{1}\in\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{\kappa,\chi}(\bm{y})
, for which there is an MFLS
\bm{\Gamma}_{1}
from
sw(\mathscr{E}(u_{1}))
to
\bm{\gamma}
with
\ell-k
additional switchings.

We have
u_{*}\in\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{\kappa,\chi}(\bm{y})
,
\mathscr{E}(u_{*})\in A
, and
sw(\mathscr{E}(u_{*}))\in\partial B
.
If
\dim\mathscr{H}_{\kappa,\chi-k}(\bm{y})=a+1
, then

(3.40)

\dim\mathscr{H}_{\kappa,\chi}(\bm{y})=a+1+k(n-2),

by the Fredholm index formula; each boundary node contributes
n-2
to the index.

On the other hand, the appearance of a boundary node along the Lagrangian
\partial_{2}T_{1}\times M
is a codimension
(n+1)-2\cdot 1=n-1
condition, since the Lagrangian is
(n+1)
-dimensional and the boundary of domain is mapped to a real
1
-dimensional curve. The incidence condition for
k
boundary nodes is
k(n-1)
-dimensional. In total, there is a dimension drop of

k(n-1)-k(n-2)=k.

This in turn forces
k=1
since
\mathscr{P}_{r}
has
\operatorname{ind}=1
by assumption.

Hence
u_{*}
has a single boundary node and either (i) has no almost constant bubbles or (ii) has an almost constant bubble with one boundary component which is attached to the main part along the single boundary node. Note that every boundary component of an almost constant bubble must have a boundary node by examining the projection
\pi
to
T
. Finally, the almost constant bubble in (ii) is not a disk and hence has negative Euler characteristic and negative Fredholm index; it therefore does not exist by (KR1). We are now in Case (i).

Step 2 (Description of
\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{\kappa,\chi}(\bm{y})
and
\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{\kappa,\chi-1}(\bm{y})
near
u_{*}
)

The following theorem is a gluing result which rephrases the theorem of Hirschi and Hugtenberg
[
HH25
]
in a slightly different setting. We note that their result generalizes the gluing result from Ekholm and Shende
[
ES19
]
, which in turn is a special case of ideas of Fukaya (unpublished) relating higher-genus curve counting in Lagrangian Floer theory and string topology. Let
U(u_{*})
be a neighborhood of
u_{*}
in
\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{\kappa,\chi}(\bm{y})
and let

Z=\{u_{1}\in\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{\kappa,\chi}(\bm{y})\mid\mbox{$u_{1}$ has a node along $\partial_{\operatorname{out}}T$}\}.

Theorem 3.4
.

There exists a neighborhood of
Z\cap U(u_{*})
in
\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{\kappa,\chi}(\bm{y})
of the form
D^{a}\times D^{n-1}
, such that
Z\cap U(u_{*})=D^{a}\times\{0\}
. Moreover,
Z\cap U(u_{*})
can be viewed as an open subset of
\partial\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{\kappa,\chi-1}(\bm{y})
.

Sketch of proof of Theorem
3.4
.

We convert the desired perturbations of a nodal curve into a smooth one into a commonly used gluing result (see for example
[
FOOO
]
which treats holomorphic curves before/after Lagrangian surgery).

Let us consider the following local model near a nodal curve:

(1)

the ambient symplectic/almost complex manifold is
\mathbb{C}^{2}
with the standard symplectic form,

(2)

the Lagrangian boundary condition is the standard
\mathbb{R}^{2}\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}
, and

(3)

the punctures of the finite energy holomorphic curves under consideration limit to chords (in a Morse-Bott family) from
S^{1}
to itself, where
S^{1}\subset S^{3}
is the Legendrian boundary at infinity of
\mathbb{R}^{1}
.

We glue two half-planes
\mathbb{H}=\{z\mid\operatorname{Im}(z)\leq 0\}\subset\mathbb{C}
along a node using the model

C_{c}\coloneqq\{(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\mid z_{1}\in\mathbb{H},z_{2}^{2}-z_{1}^{2}=c\}_{c\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}.

Since
C_{c}
maps to
\mathbb{H}
under the projection to the
z_{1}
-coordinate, we may view
C_{c}
as a subset of
\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{C}
, where
\mathbb{H}
is viewed as the
A_{\infty}
-base direction.

More generally, for
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}_{z_{1},\dots,z_{n+1}}
with
n\geq 1
, the pairs of half-planes are of the form:

\displaystyle P_{1}(z_{3},\dots,z_{n+1})\coloneqq\{(z_{1},\dots,z_{n+1})\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\mid z_{2}=z_{1},z_{1}\in\mathbb{H},z_{3},\dots,z_{n+1}\in\mathbb{R}\},

\displaystyle P_{2}\coloneqq\{(z_{1},\dots,z_{n+1})\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\mid z_{2}=-z_{1},z_{1}\in\mathbb{H},z_{3}=\dots=z_{n+1}=0\},

and the gluing of
P_{1}(z_{3},\dots,z_{n+1})
and
P_{2}
into
C_{c}
occurs when
z_{3}=\dots=z_{n+1}=0
(when
n=1
this just means there is no extra condition). The half-planes are Morse-Bott regular since they can be doubled into standard complex lines in
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
and a curve is regular if and only if its double is; the same also holds for
C_{c}
. Again we may view
P_{1}(z_{3},\dots,z_{n+1})
,
P_{2}
, and
C_{c}
as subsets of
\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
.

Next, given a nodal curve
u_{*}
, we “zoom in” near the intersection point
x\in T\times T^{*}M
and view it as a
2
-level SFT building, where the top level maps to
(T\times T^{*}M)\setminus\{x\}
and the bottom level maps to the local model just treated, with
\operatorname{dim}M=n
. The curves on the bottom level corresponding to
\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{\kappa,\chi}(\bm{y})
are pairs
(P_{1}(z_{3},\dots,z_{n+1}),P_{2})
(parametrized by
D^{n-1}
) and those corresponding to
\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{\kappa,\chi-1}(\bm{y})
are the
C_{c}
with
z_{3}=\dots=z_{n+1}=0
(parametrized by
c\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
).

Finally, it remains to verify the necessary gluing result for finite energy holomorphic curves with Lagrangian boundary in this case. Such gluing analysis is performed in
[
HH25
, Appendix A]
and we refer the reader to the proof of
[
HH25
, Theorem A.4]
for further details. The theorem then follows.
∎

Step 3 (Descriptions of
A
and
B
)
Now suppose that having an MFLS that goes to
\bm{\gamma}
is a codimension
a
condition for some
a\geq 0
. Recalling the definitions of
A
and
B
from earlier in the proof,
\dim B=1
. Theorem
3.4
, and the fact that the evaluation map
\mathscr{E}
is an immersion give:

Claim 3.5
.

There exists an identification
A\cap\mathscr{E}(U(u_{*}))\simeq D^{a}\times D^{n-1}
with respect to which
\mathscr{E}(u_{*})=(0,0)
,
\mathscr{E}(Z\cap U(u_{*}))=D^{a}\times\{0\}
, and
sw(\mathscr{E}(u_{*}))
is the unique point on
\partial B
on a neighborhood of
sw(\mathscr{E}(u_{*}))
.

The fact that
\mathscr{E}(u_{*})
for
u_{*}
, regarded as an element of
\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{\kappa,\chi-1}(\bm{y})
, coincides with
sw(\mathscr{E}(u_{*}))
for
u_{*}
, regarded as an element of
\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{\kappa,\chi}(\bm{y})
, follows from the fact that in the first case we apply
\nu_{[r]}
a total of
(b+1)
times and in the second case only
b
times when computing the evaluation map; see (
3.37
).

Step 4 (Description of
A^{\prime}
)
A standard gluing result for MFLS gives the following:

Claim 3.6
.

Under the identification from Claim
3.5
,
A^{\prime}\cap\mathscr{E}(U(u_{*}))=\{0\}\times r
, where
r
is a radial ray in
D^{n-1}
emanating from
0
.

Finally note that the number of switchings along (I, II) is
1
larger than the number along III, while
\chi(u_{1})=\chi(u_{2})+1
.
Therefore the continuation between
(u_{1},(\mathrm{I,II}))
and
(u_{2},\mathrm{III})
does not change the exponent of
\hbar
in Equation (
3.34
).

The remaining codimension
1
degenerations correspond to breakings of
u
or
\bm{\Gamma}
and are treated elsewhere in the paper. This yields
d\circ\mathscr{F}^{1}=\mathscr{F}^{1}\circ d
.
∎

3.3.4.
Quasi-isomorphism

Proposition 3.7
.

\mathscr{F}^{1}
is a quasi-isomorphism, after base changing to
R\llbracket\hbar\rrbracket
.

Proof.

Observe that
\mathscr{F}^{1}
induces a morphism between quotients
\hbar=0
:

(3.41)

\mathscr{F}^{1}|_{\hbar=0}\colon HF^{*}(\sqcup_{i}T_{q_{i}}^{*}M)|_{\hbar=0}\to HM^{*}(\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q}))|_{\hbar=0},

which is clearly a quasi-isomorphism, as it essentially coincides with the multi-loop version of the quasi-isomorphism introduced in
[
Abo12
, Section 4]
; see
[
HTY a
, Lemma 6.5]
and
[
KY23
, Lemma 4.18]
for more details.

Next, since
\mathscr{F}^{1}
is a deformation of
\mathscr{F}^{1}|_{\hbar=0}
and the latter is a quasi-isomorphism, it is a standard fact of homological algebra that
\mathscr{F}^{1}
is also a quasi-isomorphism; see e.g.,
[
KY23
, Lemma 4.19]
for a detailed proof.
∎

Remark 3.8
.

We refer the reader to
[
Her17
,
Kel06
,
Kre23
,
LB15
]
for a more in-depth discussion of the theory of deformations of
A_{\infty}
-algebras and
A_{\infty}
-categories.

3.4.
The higher-order maps
\mathscr{F}^{d}

Given
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern (C) The tuple
\bm{\Gamma}
is a Morse flow
shrub
with
\ell=r-b
switches. Explicitly, there is a tree
(T,\bm{\tau})\in\mathscr{T}^{\ell}_{w}
and

\bm{\Gamma}=(\bm{\Gamma}^{e_{1}},\dots,\bm{\Gamma}^{e_{w}},(\bm{\Gamma}^{e})_{e\in E_{\operatorname{int}}(T)},\bm{\Gamma}^{e_{0}})

is defined as in Definition
2.19
, with only (1) altered and we explain this alteration below:

We have

\displaystyle\bm{\Gamma}^{e_{i}}=((\theta_{s^{e_{i}}_{0}}^{e_{i}},\Gamma_{0}^{e_{i}},l_{0}^{e_{i}}),(\theta^{e_{i}}_{s^{e_{i}}_{1}},\Gamma_{1}^{e_{i}},l^{e_{i}}_{1})\dots,(\theta_{s^{e_{i}}_{\ell_{e_{i}}-1}}^{e_{i}},\Gamma^{e_{i}}_{\ell_{e_{i}}-1},l^{e_{i}}_{\ell_{e_{i}}-1}),(\Gamma^{e_{i}}_{\ell_{e_{i}}},l^{e_{i}}_{\ell_{e_{i}}})),

\displaystyle\Gamma_{j}^{e_{i}}\colon[0,l^{e_{i}}_{j}]\to\Omega^{1,2}(M,\bm{q}^{i-1},\bm{q}^{i}),

for each
i=1,\dots,w
and
j=0,\dots,\ell_{e_{i}}
, and we impose the switching conditions of Definition
2.8
(
4
) dictated by
\bm{\tau}_{e_{i}}
.

For each
i=1,\dots,d
, we have
\Gamma_{0}^{e_{i}}(0)=\mathscr{E}(u_{i})
.

Finally, the distance
d(v_{i},v)
from each incoming vertex
v_{i}
of the tree to the incoming vertex
v
of the outgoing edge
e_{0}
is independent of
i
. Here
d(v_{i},v)
is obtained by adding up all the lengths
l^{e}_{j}
that appear along the shortest path from
v_{i}
to
v
. See
[
Abo11
, Definition 3.1]
for more details on the description of the moduli space of shrubs
S^{\ell}_{w}
(with
\ell
marked points).

Proposition 3.10
.

\mathscr{F}
is an
A_{\infty}
-morphism, i.e., it satisfies

(3.79)

\displaystyle\sum_{w}\sum_{s_{1},\dots,s_{w}}\mu^{w}_{M}(\mathscr{F}^{s_{w}}(\bm{y}_{d},\dots,\bm{y}_{d-s_{w}+1}),\dots,\mathscr{F}^{s_{1}}(\bm{y}_{s_{1}},\dots,\bm{y}_{1}))=

\displaystyle\sum_{k,d^{\prime}}(-1)^{\text{✠}_{n}}\mathscr{F}^{d-d^{\prime}+1}(\bm{y}_{d},\dots,\bm{y}_{k+d^{\prime}+1},\mu^{d^{\prime}}_{F}(\bm{y}_{k+d^{\prime}},\dots,\bm{y}_{k+1}),\bm{y}_{k},\dots,\bm{y}_{1}).

has

). The example of

. We also refer the reader to

, Lemma 4.8]
.

3.79
). The example of
\mathscr{F}^{5}(\bm{y}_{6},\dots,\mu^{2}(\bm{y}_{4},\bm{y}_{3}),\dots,\bm{y}_{1})
is illustrated on the left-hand side of Figure
3.4
.

Figure 3.4
.
Some boundary strata of
\mathscr{P}_{r}(\vec{\bm{y}},\bm{\gamma})
.

(3) Going from the left to the middle of Figure
3.5
corresponds to a boundary degeneration which is already seen on the level of
\mathscr{H}^{d}
, corresponding to the pinching along an arc connecting
\partial_{\operatorname{out}}T
to some
\partial_{i}T
. Going from the right to the middle corresponds to the contraction of two incoming edges with a common outgoing vertex. Both of these gluing results are described in detail in a similar situation for
\kappa=1
in
[
AS10
, Section 6.4]
and we refer the reader to it for a detailed analysis.

Figure 3.5
.
Breaking along the zero section.

(4) The last possible scenario is a branch point on the
u_{i}
side (for some
1\leq i\leq w
) becoming a switching point on the shrub side (and vice versa). This is treated in Theorem
3.3
.

The remainder of the proof consists of a very tedious sign verification that we omit here. We note that correctness of the sign
\dagger(\bm{u})
can be checked solely by analyzing breakings of type (2) and in an analogous situation it is carried out in
[
Abo11
, Section 8.3]
(note that symbolically it is the same computation). The verification of the sign

.
∎

Theorem 3.11
.

\mathscr{F}
induces a quasi-equivalence of
\hbar
-adic completions.

Proof.

This follows from Propositions
3.7
and
3.10
.
∎

4.
The based multiloop
A_{\infty}
-algebra of
S^{2}

The goal of this section is to prove basic properties of the dga
H_{\kappa}
from Definition
1.8
in Section
4.1
and to prove Theorem
1.7
in Sections
4.2
–
4.5
.

We point out that given an
A_{\infty}
-algebra
(A,\{\mu^{d}\}_{d\geq 1})
with vanishing
\mu^{d}
for
d\geq 3
there is a naturally associated dga
(A,d,\cdot)
with differential and multiplication given via

d(a)=\mu^{1}(a),\quad a*b=(-1)^{|a|}\mu^{2}(b,a).

In this section we call a tuple
(A,\mu^{1},\mu^{2})
arising from an
A_{\infty}
-algebra
(A,\{\mu^{d}\}_{d\geq 1})
as above a dga by abuse of notation, while understanding that to obtain an actual dga one needs to pass through this assignment. In particular, the dga
H_{\kappa}
is obtained via such an assignment from
A_{\infty}
-algebra to which as we claim the
A_{\infty}
-algebra
CM_{-*}(S^{2},\bm{q})
is quasi-equivalent.

4.1.
Basic properties of
H_{\kappa}

The subalgebra
H_{\kappa}^{\operatorname{fin}}
of
H_{\kappa}
generated by the
T_{i}
is isomorphic to the finite Hecke algebra associated to the symmetric group
S_{\kappa}
.
In particular,
T_{i}^{-1}=T_{i}-\hbar 1
. The generator
x_{1}
gives a homotopy between

T_{1}T_{2}\cdots T_{\kappa-2}T_{\kappa-1}^{2}T_{\kappa-2}\cdots T_{2}T_{1}\quad\mbox{and}\quad 1.

Remark 4.1
.

There is a generalization
H_{\kappa}(c)
with a central parameter
c
, where the only difference is that the differential becomes

(4.1)

dx_{1}=T_{1}T_{2}\cdots T_{\kappa-2}T_{\kappa-1}^{2}T_{\kappa-2}\cdots T_{2}T_{1}-c.

Lemma 4.2
.

The dga
H_{\kappa}
is well-defined.

Proof.

It suffices to verify that the differential
d
preserves the defining relations.
This is obvious for the first three relations (
1.3
), (
1.4
), (
1.5
) since
dT_{i}=0
.

For the fourth relation (
1.6
), we have

\displaystyle d(x_{1}T_{j})=

\displaystyle T_{1}T_{2}\cdots T_{\kappa-2}T_{\kappa-1}^{2}T_{\kappa-2}\cdots T_{2}T_{1}T_{j}-T_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{1}\cdots T_{\kappa-2}T_{\kappa-1}^{2}T_{\kappa-2}\cdots T_{j}T_{j-1}T_{j}T_{j-2}\cdots T_{1}-T_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{1}\cdots T_{\kappa-2}T_{\kappa-1}^{2}T_{\kappa-2}\cdots T_{j-1}T_{j}T_{j-1}T_{j-2}\cdots T_{1}-T_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{1}\cdots T_{j-2}T_{j-1}T_{j}T_{j-1}\cdots T_{\kappa-2}T_{\kappa-1}^{2}T_{\kappa-2}\cdots T_{1}-T_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{1}\cdots T_{j-2}T_{j}T_{j-1}T_{j}\cdots T_{\kappa-2}T_{\kappa-1}^{2}T_{\kappa-2}\cdots T_{1}-T_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{j}T_{1}\cdots\cdots T_{\kappa-2}T_{\kappa-1}^{2}T_{\kappa-2}\cdots T_{1}-T_{j}=d(T_{j}x_{1}).

For the last relation (
1.7
), let
T^{\prime}=T_{2}\cdots T_{\kappa-2}T_{\kappa-1}^{2}T_{\kappa-2}\cdots T_{2}
which commutes with
x_{1}
. We have

\displaystyle d(T_{1}^{-1}x_{1}T_{1}^{-1}x_{1}+x_{1}T_{1}^{-1}x_{1}T_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{1}^{-1}(T_{1}T^{\prime}T_{1}-1)T_{1}^{-1}x_{1}-T_{1}^{-1}x_{1}T_{1}^{-1}(T_{1}T^{\prime}T_{1}-1)

\displaystyle+(T_{1}T^{\prime}T_{1}-1)T_{1}^{-1}x_{1}T_{1}-x_{1}T_{1}^{-1}(T_{1}T^{\prime}T_{1}-1)T_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T^{\prime}x_{1}-T_{1}^{-2}x_{1}-T_{1}^{-1}x_{1}T^{\prime}T_{1}+T_{1}^{-1}x_{1}T_{1}^{-1}+T_{1}T^{\prime}x_{1}T_{1}-T_{1}^{-1}x_{1}T_{1}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-x_{1}T^{\prime}T_{1}^{2}+x_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x_{1}-T_{1}^{-2}x_{1})+(T_{1}^{-1}x_{1}T_{1}^{-1}-T_{1}^{-1}x_{1}T_{1})+(T^{\prime}x_{1}-x_{1}T^{\prime}T_{1}^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+(-T_{1}^{-1}x_{1}T^{\prime}T_{1}+T_{1}T^{\prime}x_{1}T_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hbar T_{1}^{-1}x_{1}+(-T_{1}^{-1}x_{1}\hbar)+(-x_{1}T^{\prime}\hbar T_{1})+\hbar x_{1}T^{\prime}T_{1}=0,

where the last equality uses the quadratic relation
T_{1}^{2}-1=\hbar T_{1},T_{1}-T_{1}^{-1}=\hbar
.
∎

There is an alternate formulation of
H_{\kappa}
which is obtained by inductively introducing generators
x_{2},\dots,x_{\kappa}
as follows:

(4.2)

x_{k+1}=T_{k}^{-1}x_{k}T_{k}^{-1},\quad 1\leq k\leq\kappa-1.

We have

(4.3)

\displaystyle T_{k}x_{k}=T_{k}^{-1}x_{k}+\hbar x_{k}=x_{k+1}T_{k}+\hbar x_{k},

(4.4)

\displaystyle T_{k}x_{k+1}=x_{k}T_{k}^{-1}=x_{k}T_{k}-\hbar x_{k},

(4.5)

\displaystyle T_{k}x_{j}=x_{j}T_{k},\quad j\neq k,k+1.

The anticommutator relation (
1.7
) becomes
x_{2}x_{1}+x_{1}x_{2}T_{1}^{2}=0
and a direct computation shows that

(4.6)

\displaystyle x_{k}x_{j}+x_{j}x_{k}(T_{j-1}\cdots T_{1})(T_{k-1}\cdots T_{2})T_{1}^{2}(T_{2}^{-1}\cdots T_{k-1}^{-1})(T_{1}^{-1}\cdots T_{j-1}^{-1})=0,

holds for
1\leq j<k\leq\kappa
.

We now state and prove some properties of
H_{\kappa}
. The first concerns the specialization of
H_{\kappa}
at
\hbar=0
.

Lemma 4.3
.

H_{\kappa}|_{\hbar=0}\cong\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\dots,x_{\kappa}]\rtimes S_{\kappa}
,
where
\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\dots,x_{\kappa}]
denotes the graded polynomial ring, i.e.,
x_{k}x_{j}+x_{j}x_{k}=0,j\neq k,
and
|x_{i}|=-1
.

Proof.

This follows from noting that (i) the subalgebra
H_{\kappa}^{\operatorname{fin}}|_{\hbar=0}
is the group ring
\mathbb{Z}[S_{\kappa}]
; (ii) the differential becomes trivial since
dx_{1}=0
; and (iii) the anticommutator relation (
4.6
) reduces to
x_{k}x_{j}+x_{j}x_{k}=0
for
1\leq j<k\leq\kappa
.
∎

The second concerns the zeroth cohomology
H^{0}(H_{\kappa})
.

Lemma 4.4
.

There is an isomorphism of rings:

(4.7)

H^{0}(H_{\kappa})\cong\mathbb{Z}[\operatorname{Br}_{\kappa}(S^{2})]/\{T_{i}^{2}=1+\hbar T_{i}\}.

Hence
H^{0}(H_{\kappa})
is isomorphic to the braid skein algebra of
S^{2}
.

Proof.

It is easy to see that
H^{0}(H_{\kappa})
is a quotient of the finite Hecke algebra
H_{\kappa}^{\operatorname{fin}}
by the relation

(4.8)

T_{1}T_{2}\cdots T_{\kappa-2}T_{\kappa-1}^{2}T_{\kappa-2}\cdots T_{2}T_{1}-1=0.

By replacing
T_{i}
with the half twist along the
i
th and
(i+1)
st points on the sphere
S^{2}
, the corresponding relation is indeed that of the defining relation of the braid group
\operatorname{Br}_{\kappa}(S^{2})
of
S^{2}
.
∎

Finally we present a linear basis of
H_{\kappa}
of PBW type.

Lemma 4.5
.

The algebra
H_{\kappa}
has a free basis
x^{\alpha}T_{w}
over
\mathbb{Z}[\hbar]
, where:

•

x^{\alpha}=x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{\kappa}^{\alpha_{\kappa}}
, for
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\kappa}),\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and

•

T_{w},w\in S_{\kappa}
is a free
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
H_{\kappa}^{\operatorname{fin}}
.

Proof.

The relations (
4.3
), (
4.4
), and (
4.5
) imply that one can move
T_{i}
from the left of
x_{j}
to the right.
By the relation (
4.6
) one can move
x_{j}
in front of
x_{k}
for all
j<k
in a product of
x_{i}
’s.
Hence the collection
x^{\alpha}T_{w}
spans
H_{\kappa}
.

The linear independence follows from observing that the collection
x^{\alpha}T_{w}|_{\hbar=0}
is a free
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
H_{\kappa}|_{\hbar=0}
.
∎

4.2.
Verification of (
1.3
)–(
1.8
)

We begin the proof of Theorem
1.7
by verifying (
1.3
)–(
1.8
) of Definition
1.8
for the HDHF
A_{\infty}
-algebra for the standard round metric on
S^{2}
and certain positions of endpoints.

We will use the fact that
\mu^{3}_{M}
vanishes for certain tuples of total degree greater than
-3
.

In Section
4.3
we show the vanishing of
\mu_{M}^{d}
,
d\geq 3
, for tuples of total degree greater than
-N\ll 0
, for a suitable Finsler modification of the metric, and a similar argument shows the vanishing of
\mu^{3}_{M}
for the round metric on
S^{2}
in the cases we need. Then in Sections
4.4
and
4.5
we construct a quasi-isomorphism of the HDHF
A_{\infty}
-algebra with the dga
H_{\kappa}
by a direct limit argument.

Let
d
be the standard round metric on
S^{2}
. For all the computations in Section
4.2
we will use the standard energy functional

\mathscr{A}(\gamma)=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{2}|\dot{\gamma}(t)|^{2}dt.

The standard action functional with the round metric behaves poorly with products. Instead of perturbing
d
, we can get products to behave generically by taking disjoint copies
{\bm{q}},{\bm{q}}^{\prime},{\bm{q}}^{\prime\prime}
of the basepoints while requiring that no
3
points lie on a geodesic. In what follows, the points
{\bm{q}},{\bm{q}}^{\prime},{\bm{q}}^{\prime\prime}
clearly violate this, but it is to be understood that we further make a small perturbation of
{\bm{q}},{\bm{q}}^{\prime},{\bm{q}}^{\prime\prime}
so that no
3
points are collinear.

Description of the basepoints.
Fix
\varepsilon>0
small. Given points
a\not=b\in S^{2}
that are not antipodal, let
g(a,b)
be the shortest geodesic connecting
a
and
b
. Pick basepoints
{\bm{q}}=(q_{1},\dots,q_{\kappa})\subset S^{2}
, arranged in order on a short geodesic
g
, and their pushoffs
{\bm{q}}^{\prime}=(q_{1}^{\prime},\dots,q_{\kappa}^{\prime})
,
{\bm{q}}^{\prime\prime}=(q_{1}^{\prime\prime},\dots,q_{\kappa}^{\prime\prime})\subset S^{2}
, such that the following hold:

(1)

There exists
\tilde{q}\in S^{2}
near
q_{1}
such that
\tilde{q},q_{i},q_{i}^{\prime},q_{i}^{\prime\prime}
lie on a geodesic
g_{i}
in that order for
i=1,\dots,\kappa
, moreover
g_{1}
is orthogonal to
g
; in the actual perturbation we push each
q_{i}^{\prime\prime}
to the right of the corresponding geodesic
g_{i}
.

(2)

Let
s_{i}
be the point of intersection of
g(q_{i},q_{i+1}^{\prime})
and
g(q_{i+1},q_{i}^{\prime})
. Then
d(q_{i},s_{i})>d(q_{i+1},s_{i})
and
d(q_{i},q_{i+1}^{\prime})<d(q_{i+1},q_{i}^{\prime})
for
i=1,\dots,\kappa-1
. An analogous condition holds for points of intersection of
g(q_{i}^{\prime},q_{i+1}^{\prime\prime})
and
g(q_{i+1}^{\prime},q_{i}^{\prime\prime})
.

(3)

For
i=1,\dots,\kappa
we have
d(q_{i},q_{i}^{\prime}),d(q_{i},q_{i}^{\prime\prime})<\varepsilon
, and for
i=1,\dots,\kappa-1
we have
d(q_{i},q_{i}^{\prime})>d(q_{i+1},q_{i+1}^{\prime})
,
d(q_{i},q_{i}^{\prime\prime})>d(q_{i+1},q_{i+1}^{\prime\prime})
,

\tfrac{d(\tilde{q},q_{i+1})}{2\pi-d(q_{i+1},q_{i+1}^{\prime})}>\tfrac{d(\tilde{q},q_{i})}{2\pi-d(q_{i},q_{i}^{\prime})}
,

\tfrac{d(\tilde{q},q_{i+1})}{2\pi-d(q_{i+1},q_{i+1}^{\prime\prime})}>\tfrac{d(\tilde{q},q_{i})}{2\pi-d(q_{i},q_{i}^{\prime\prime})}
. (Here
2\pi
is the length of the equator and
2\pi-d(q_{i},q_{i}^{\prime})
is the length of the second shortest geodesic from
q_{i}
to
q_{i}^{\prime}
.)

See Figure
4.1

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

Figure 4.1
.
The points of
{\bm{q}}
,
{\bm{q}}^{\prime}
, and
{\bm{q}}^{\prime\prime}
.

We point out that we actually compute

\mu_{M}^{1}\colon CM_{-*}(\Omega(S^{2},{\bm{q}},{\bm{q}^{\prime}}))\to CM_{-*}(\Omega(S^{2},{\bm{q}},{\bm{q}^{\prime}})),

\mu_{M}^{2}\colon CM_{-*}(\Omega(S^{2},{\bm{q}},{\bm{q}^{\prime}}))\otimes CM_{-*}(\Omega(S^{2},{\bm{q}^{\prime}},{\bm{q}^{\prime\prime}}))\to CM_{-*}(\Omega(S^{2},{\bm{q}},{\bm{q}^{\prime\prime}})).

The result is quasi-equivalent to the computations in the
A_{\infty}
-algebra
CM_{-*}(\Omega(S^{2},{\bm{q}}))
itself with the
A_{\infty}
-morphism induced by isotoping tuples of points
{\bm{q}^{\prime}}
and
{\bm{q}^{\prime\prime}}
to
{\bm{q}}
. The elements of
CM_{-*}(\Omega(S^{2},{\bm{q}},{\bm{q}^{\prime}}))
,
CM_{-*}(\Omega(S^{2},{\bm{q}^{\prime}},{\bm{q}^{\prime\prime}}))
, and
CM_{-*}(\Omega(S^{2},{\bm{q}^{\prime}},{\bm{q}^{\prime\prime}}))
are formally identified via the obvious bijections.

Description of generators of
CM_{-*}(\Omega(S^{2},\bm{q}))
as a
\mathbb{Z}[\hbar]
-module.
We are viewing
CM_{-*}(\Omega(S^{2},\bm{q}))
as being generated by
\kappa
-tuples of geodesics from
{\bm{q}}
to
{\bm{q}}^{\prime}
.

Let
[T_{i}]=T_{i}
(by abuse of notation) be the generator of degree
0
consisting of shortest geodesics
g(q_{j},q_{j}^{\prime})
for
j\not=i,i+1
and shortest geodesics
g(q_{i},q_{i+1}^{\prime})
and
g(q_{i+1},q_{i}^{\prime})
(note that since both of these geodesics are of index
0
the orientation line

The degree

Let

According to our sign conventions, for any
[\bm{y}]=[y_{1}]\otimes\dots\otimes[y_{\kappa}]\in CM_{-*}(\Omega(S^{2},{\bm{q}},{\bm{q}^{\prime}}))

Verification of (
1.3
)–(
1.5
).
We verify (
1.3
); the proofs of (
1.4
) and (
1.5
) are analogous but easier. We use the notation
\bm{\gamma}_{0}\sharp\bm{\gamma}_{1}

to denote the concatenation of multipaths
\bm{\gamma}_{0}
and
\bm{\gamma}_{1}
. Notice that since all the involved generators in (
1.3
)–(
1.5
) are of degree
0
the product associated with
\mu_{M}^{2}
as described in the beginning of this section has the same signs.
First observe that since
T_{i}^{2}
has degree
0
, the MFLS starting from
T_{i}\sharp T_{i}
without switches eventually arrives at a collection of shortest geodesics corresponding to
1
. The case of an MFLS with a single switch would then contribute
\hbar T_{i}
.

In general, let
\bm{\gamma}
be a non-geodesic multipath for which there is a rigid (=isolated) MFLS from
T_{i}\sharp T_{i}
to
\bm{\gamma}
satisfying the conditions of Definition
2.8
, except that
\Gamma_{\ell}:[0,l_{\ell}]\to\Omega^{1,2}(S^{2},\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime\prime})
is a finite-length trajectory and
\Gamma_{\ell}(l_{\ell})=\bm{\gamma}
. Also let
\operatorname{CR}(\bm{\gamma})
(short for
complete resolution
of
\bm{\gamma}
) be given by the analog of the right-hand side of Equation (
2.30
), where we are now counting (with
\hbar
-terms) rigid MFLS from
\bm{\gamma}
satisfying the conditions of Definition
2.8
, except that
\Gamma_{0}:[l_{0},0]\to\Omega^{1,2}(S^{2},\bm{q},\bm{q}^{\prime\prime})
is a finite-length trajectory and
\Gamma_{0}(l_{0})=\bm{\gamma}
. Observe that
\operatorname{CR}(\bm{\gamma})
can be written uniquely as a
\mathbb{Z}\llbracket\hbar\rrbracket
-linear combination of
T_{\sigma}
,
\sigma\in S_{\kappa}
.

Now suppose
\bm{\gamma}
occurs before a crossing and
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
(resp.
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}
) is the multipath obtained by flowing without any crossings until one is reached, passing through the crossing (resp. resolving at the crossing), and flowing without any further crossings. Note that
\bm{\gamma}
,
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
, and
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}
are not necessarily generators of
CM_{-*}(\Omega(S^{2},\bm{q}))
. Let
[\cdot]_{H}
denote the equivalence class in
\mathbb{Z}[\hbar,\hbar^{-1}]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}B_{\kappa}
modulo isotopy and the HOMFLY skein relations. Then:

(*)

[\bm{\gamma}]_{H}=[\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}]_{H}\pm\hbar[\bm{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}]_{H}
and
\operatorname{CR}(\bm{\gamma})=\operatorname{CR}(\bm{\gamma}^{\prime})\pm\hbar\operatorname{CR}(\bm{\gamma}^{\prime\prime})
.

Starting “at the end” with
\bm{\gamma}
,
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
, and
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}
such that
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
and
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}
are geodesic, we have
\operatorname{CR}(\bm{\gamma}^{\prime})=\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
and
\operatorname{CR}(\bm{\gamma}^{\prime\prime})=\bm{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}
and hence
[\operatorname{CR}(\bm{\gamma})]_{H}=[\bm{\gamma}]_{H}
by (*). Working backwards, we inductively establish that
[\operatorname{CR}(\bm{\gamma})]_{H}=[\bm{\gamma}]_{H}
for any
\bm{\gamma}
. Finally, specializing to the case where there is an MFLS from
T_{i}\sharp T_{i}
to
\bm{\gamma}
with no crossings or switches,
T_{i}^{2}=[\operatorname{CR}(\bm{\gamma})]_{H}=[\bm{\gamma}]_{H}
, which implies (
1.3
).

Verification of (
1.8
).
As in Example
2.14
, in the calculation of
\mu^{1}_{M}(x_{1})
there are two MFLS from
x_{1}
to
1
: one where the corresponding
1
-parameter family of paths from
q_{1}
to
q_{1}^{\prime}
does not intersect any
q_{i}
for
i>1
(i.e., it is to the right of the geodesic of degree
-1
from
q_{1}
to
q_{1}^{\prime}
) and another one that flows through a multipath
\bm{\gamma}
consisting of a small loop from
q_{1}
to
q_{1}^{\prime}
(after isotoping
q_{1}^{\prime}
to
q_{1}
) which encircles all the
q_{i}
,
i>1
, together with short geodesics from
q_{i}
to
q_{i}^{\prime}
for
i>1
; see Figure
4.3
. The multipath
\bm{\gamma}
is homotopic as a braid to

T_{1}T_{2}\dots T_{\kappa-2}T^{2}_{\kappa-1}T_{\kappa-2}\dots T_{2}T_{1}

and hence in view of (*) we obtain

\mu^{1}_{M}(x_{1})=T_{1}T_{2}\dots T_{\kappa-2}T^{2}_{\kappa-1}T_{\kappa-2}\dots T_{2}T_{1}-1.

This implies (
1.8
).

Figure 4.3
.
The generator
1
is in green and the generator
x
is in blue. The curves in the unstable manifold of

The relation
dT_{i}=0
follows immediately by noting that
|T_{i}|=0
and has the highest degree.

Verification of (
1.6
).
This follows from an explicit proof of the fact that
\mu^{2}_{M}(x,1)=-\mu^{2}_{M}(1,x)=x
in the usual
CM_{-*}(\Omega(S^{2},q))
with
\kappa=1
, where
x
is a degree
-1
generator and the sign change comes from (
4.9
): Referring to Figure
4.3
, the compactification
\overline{W}_{u}(x)
of the unstable manifold of
x
is homeomorphic to
S^{1}
and
\overline{W}_{u}(x)\setminus\{1,x\}
consists of the “left” and “right” portions
W^{L}(x)
and
W^{R}(x)
, depending on whether a given curve is contained in the left or right hemisphere with respect to the geodesic path
x
. Then the flow from
\gamma_{L}\in W^{L}(x)
approaches
1
from the right, while the flow from
\gamma_{R}\in W^{R}
approaches it from the left. Now consider the flow starting from the concatenation
\gamma_{L}\sharp 1
. If
\gamma_{L}
is sufficiently to the left, then the flow approaches
1
from the right. Likewise, if
\gamma_{R}
is sufficiently to the right, then the flow from
\gamma_{R}\sharp 1
approaches
1
from the left. Therefore, by continuity the algebraic count of trajectories from
\tilde{\gamma}\sharp 1
,
\tilde{\gamma}\in\overline{W}_{u}(x)\setminus\{1,x\}
, to
x
is
1
(for example, if there was no such flow this would contradict the continuity of the side from which the flow is approaching).

The flow tree starting from
x_{1}=x\otimes 1\otimes\dots\otimes 1
and
T_{j}=1\otimes\dots\otimes 1\otimes T^{\kappa=2}_{j}\otimes 1\otimes\dots\otimes 1
decomposes into the flow tree for concatenating
1
and
x
, viewed as generators for
\kappa=1
, and the flow tree corresponding to the concatenation of
1\otimes 1
and
T^{\kappa=2}_{j}
, viewed as generators for
\kappa=2
. The same can be said about flow tree with order of the above generators reversed, hence
\mu^{2}_{M}(x_{1},T_{j})=-\mu^{2}_{M}(T_{j},x_{1})
implying (
1.6
) for the associated multiplication.

Verification of (
4.2
).
We show that
\mu^{2}_{M}(T_{1},\mu^{2}_{M}(x_{2},T_{1}))=-x_{1}
; the situation for larger
k
is analogous.
In the composition
\mu^{2}_{M}(x_{2},T_{1})
there is a single concatenation of a geodesic of type
x
with a short geodesic; in view of the multiplication in the usual
CM_{-*}(\Omega(S^{2},q))
with
\kappa=1
, the flow tree from the pair consisting of the type
x
geodesic and the short geodesic limits to a geodesic of type
x
from
q_{2}
to
q_{1}^{\prime}
. There are no crossings in this case and hence no
\hbar
term. The same consideration also holds for
\mu_{M}^{2}(T_{1},\mu^{2}_{M}(x_{2},T_{1}))
, and hence
\mu^{2}_{M}(T_{1},\mu^{2}_{M}(x_{2},T_{1}))=-x_{1}
. This, given the associativity, implies (
4.2
) for the associated multiplication.

).
We claim that

\displaystyle x_{2}x_{1}+x_{1}x_{2}T_{1}^{2}=x_{2}x_{1}+x_{1}x_{2}+\hbar x_{1}x_{2}T_{1}=0,\text{ or equivalently}

\displaystyle\mu^{2}_{M}(x_{1},x_{2})+\mu^{2}_{M}(T_{1}^{2},\mu^{2}_{M}(x_{2},x_{1}))=\mu_{M}^{2}(x_{1},x_{2})+\mu^{2}_{M}(1+\hbar T_{1},\mu_{M}^{2}(x_{2},x_{1}))=0.

The proof of
\mu_{M}^{2}(x_{1},x_{2})=\bm{\gamma}+\hbar\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
is involved and will occupy the next several pages. Assuming this, we proceed as follows:
We claim that
\mu^{2}_{M}(T_{1},\bm{\gamma})=\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
and
\mu^{2}_{M}(T_{1},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime})=\bm{\gamma}+\hbar\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
. The first claim is straightforward and the second is computed as follows, assuming associativity:

\mu^{2}_{M}(T_{1},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime})=\mu^{2}_{M}(T_{1},\mu^{2}_{M}(T_{1},\bm{\gamma}))=\mu^{2}_{M}(1+\hbar T_{1},\bm{\gamma})=\bm{\gamma}+\hbar\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}.

Combining all of the above, we obtain:

\mu^{2}_{M}(x_{1},x_{2})+\mu^{2}_{M}(1+\hbar T_{1},\mu^{2}_{M}(x_{2},x_{1}))=(\bm{\gamma}+\hbar\bm{\gamma}^{\prime})+\mu_{M}^{2}(1+\hbar T_{1},-\bm{\gamma})=(\bm{\gamma}+\hbar\bm{\gamma}^{\prime})-\bm{\gamma}-\hbar\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}=0.

Verification of
\mu_{M}^{2}(x_{1},x_{2})=\bm{\gamma}+\hbar\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
.
Clearly there is an MFTS from
(x_{1},x_{2})
without switchings to
\bm{\gamma}
. The sign is positive now because in the canonical isomorphism (

) for this MFTS the order of

and

is switched as opposed to the one associated with the only MFTS contributing to

We now show that there is an MFTS with
1
switching from
(x_{1},x_{2})
to
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
. Let
\tilde{\gamma}_{2}

0

\tilde{\gamma}_{1}

and

Claim 4.6
.

For sufficiently small
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
, there exists a moment
s=s_{0}
on the Morse flow line
\bm{\Gamma}:[0,\infty)\to\Omega(S^{2},(q_{1},q_{2}),(q_{1}^{\prime\prime},q_{2}^{\prime\prime}))
starting at
(1,\gamma_{2}(z_{2}))\sharp(\gamma_{1}(z_{1}),1)
, when a crossing of the two strands occurs. Moreover this crossing is derived from the initial intersection point
\tilde{q}
.

By
derived from

\tilde{q}
we mean that there is a continuous family of intersection points of
\operatorname{Im}\bm{\Gamma}(s)
,
[0,s_{0}]
, which connects
\tilde{q}
and such a crossing.

The key point is that
\tilde{\gamma}_{1},\tilde{\gamma}_{2}
intersect
asynchronously
at
\tilde{q}\in\operatorname{Im}(\tilde{\gamma}_{1})\cap\operatorname{Im}(\tilde{\gamma}_{2})
, i.e.,
t_{1}\not=t_{2}
where
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}(t_{1})=\tilde{\gamma}_{2}(t_{1})=a
, whereas the claim guarantees the existence of some
s=s_{0}
where the intersection of the two paths of
\bm{\Gamma}(s_{0})
is
synchronous
.

Proof.

Observe that the flow line
\bm{\Gamma}
ends at
\bm{\gamma}
. Along
\bm{\Gamma}
the braid representative goes from the one on the top left to the one on the top right of Figure
4.4
, implying a crossing.
This crossing is derived from
\tilde{q}
and hence the claim for
(z_{1},z_{2})=(0,0)\in[0,1]_{z_{1}}\times[0,1]_{x_{2}}
holds. By continuity there exists a crossing derived from
\tilde{q}
for each
(z_{1},z_{2})
in a small neighborhood of
(0,0)\in[0,1]\times[0,1]
.
∎

Let
U_{\tilde{q}}
be the connected subset consisting of all
(z_{1},z_{2})\in[0,1]\times[0,1]
satisfying Claim
4.6
. For each
(z_{1},z_{2})\in U_{\tilde{q}}
there exists an MFTS with
1
switching derived from
\tilde{q}
. Let
\bm{\Gamma}(z_{1},z_{2})
be the flow line starting at
(z_{1},z_{2})
and let

be the moment given by Claim
4.6
when the crossing derived from
\tilde{q}
occurs. We write
\bm{\Gamma}(z_{1},z_{2})(s_{0})=(\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}_{1}(z_{1},z_{2}),\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}_{2}(z_{1},z_{2}))
. Let
(\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}_{1}(z_{1},z_{2}),\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}_{2}(z_{1},z_{2}))
be the result of applying the switching map; then
\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}_{1}(z_{1},z_{2})
(resp.
\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}_{2}(z_{1},z_{2})
) is approximately the concatenation
a_{1}(z_{1},z_{2})\sharp b_{2}(z_{1},z_{2})
(resp.
a_{2}(z_{1},z_{2})\sharp b_{1}(z_{1},z_{2})
), where we write
\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}_{i}(z_{1},z_{2})=a_{i}(z_{1},z_{2})\sharp b_{i}(z_{1},z_{2})
.

.

We also claim that there exists a unique value
z_{1}^{\prime}
such that for

there is no such MFTS with

(z_{1},0)\in[0,1]\times[0,1]

has the triangular shape as in Figure

Following the proof strategy of
\mu^{2}_{M}(x,1)=x
(see the paragraph on the verification of (
1.6
) above), we now analyze how the various MFTS approach the generator
(1,1)
, represented by geodesics
g(q_{1},q_{2}^{\prime\prime})
and
g(q_{2},q_{1}^{\prime\prime})
. For each of these generators there are two options: either a flow line approaches it from the right, or from the left. Hence there are
4
possible types of flow lines from
(x_{1},x_{2})
which we denote by
rr,rl,lr,ll
, where the first (resp. second) letter corresponds to the geodesic
g(q_{1},q_{2}^{\prime\prime})
(resp.
g(q_{2},q_{1}^{\prime\prime})
).

Claim 4.7
.

The pair
((1,\tilde{\gamma}_{2}),(\tilde{\gamma}_{1},1))
corresponding to
(z_{1},z_{2})=(0,0)
is of type
lr
.

Proof.

Let
(\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}_{1},\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}_{2})=(\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}_{1}(0,0),\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}_{2}(0,0))
; see Figure
4.6
. Note that
\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}_{2}
are very close to the generators
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
. Applying the switching map,
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}^{\prime\prime}=\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}_{1}(0,0)
and
\tilde{\gamma}_{2}^{\prime\prime}=\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}_{2}(0,0)
are approximately concatenations
a_{1}(0,0)\sharp b_{2}(0,0)
,
a_{2}(0,0)\sharp b_{1}(0,0)
which are very close to two geodesics that intersect at an angle slightly less than
\pi
. The gradient vector at
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}^{\prime}
, regarded as a vector field along
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}^{\prime}
, has a relatively small norm away from this angle, whereas near the angle its norm is significantly greater than
0
and the vector points into the interior of this angle; see
[
Mil63
, Chapter 12]
for a more precise description. Hence
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
is pushed away to the right along the flow line as shown in Figure
4.6
and the flow line approaches
g(q_{1},q_{2}^{\prime\prime})
from the left. Similarly, the flow line starting at
\tilde{\gamma}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
approaches
g(q_{2},q_{1}^{\prime\prime})
from the right.
∎

Claim 4.8
.

The points on
I_{1}
near
(z_{1}^{\prime},0)
are of type
ll
and the points on
I_{2}
near
(0,z_{2}^{\prime})
are of type
rr
.

Proof.

We prove the first assertion; the second is similar. Let
(\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}_{1},\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}_{2})=(\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}_{1}(z_{1},0),\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}_{2}(z_{1},0))
. The curve
\tilde{\gamma}_{2}^{\prime\prime}=\tilde{\gamma}_{2}^{\prime\prime}(z_{1},0)
is approximately the concatenation
a_{2}(z_{1},0)\sharp b_{1}(z_{1},0)
. Since the curve
\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}_{1}
is “sufficiently” far away from the equator, the pseudogradient vector field has “sufficiently” large norm away from the ends, so even though
\tilde{\gamma}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
has acceleration near the crossing pointing into the angle, it is not enough to pull the curve in that direction. One may also see this by arranging that the curve
\tilde{\gamma}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
is contained entirely in a hemisphere cut out by the equator passing through
q_{2}
and
q_{1}^{\prime\prime}
. Figure
4.7
gives the flow line for this case.
∎

Figure 4.7
.
We use the same conventions as in Figure
4.6
. The curves in gray show the various stages of the flow line starting at
\tilde{\gamma}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
obtained from
(\tilde{\gamma}_{1}^{\prime},\tilde{\gamma}_{2}^{\prime})
via the switching map.

By Claims
4.7
and
4.8
, along
I_{1}
(resp.
I_{2}
) there is a unique point
(z_{1}^{\prime\prime},0)
(resp.
(0,z_{2}^{\prime\prime})
) such that corresponding MFTS flows to the generator
x_{2}^{\prime}=(1,x)
(resp.
x_{1}^{\prime}=(x,1)
) of index
(-1)
and permutation type
(12)
. Let
S_{1}
(resp.
S_{2}
) be the subset of
U_{\tilde{q}}
consisting of points
(z_{1},z_{2})
such that corresponding MFTS approaches either
x_{2}^{\prime}
(resp.
x_{1}^{\prime}
) or
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
. Then
(z_{1}^{\prime\prime},0)\in S_{1}
and
(0,z_{2}^{\prime\prime})\in S_{2}
.

Now we analyze the third side
I_{3}
, which is given by the graph of a continuous monotone function. The defining condition for a point
(z_{1},z_{2})
to belong to
I_{3}
is that at the switching moment the corresponding curves
\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}_{2}
are tangent at the crossing derived from
\tilde{q}
. Figure
4.8
shows the boundary points
(z_{1}^{\prime},0)
and
(0,z_{2}^{\prime})
of
I_{3}
, which are of types
ll
and
rr
by Claim
4.8
.

Claim 4.9
.

Along
I_{3}
there is a unique point
P_{1}\in S_{1}
and a unique point
P_{2}\in S_{2}
such that
z_{1}(P_{1})<z_{1}(P_{2})
.

Proof.

For each
(z_{1},z_{2})\in I_{3}
, the point of tangency of
\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}_{1}(z_{1},z_{2})
and
\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}_{2}(z_{1},z_{2})
is closer to
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
than to
q_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and
q_{2}^{\prime\prime}
. For this reason, there exists
(z_{1},z_{2})\in I_{3}
where the average acceleration (i.e., the average norm of the pseudogradient vector) of
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(z_{1},z_{2})
along
a_{1}(z_{1},z_{2})
is smaller than that of
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(z_{1},z_{2})
along
b_{2}(z_{1},z_{2})
, but the flow approaches
g(q_{1},q_{2}^{\prime\prime})
from the right. Here one should think of the acceleration vector along
a_{1}(z_{1},z_{2})
as pointing downwards and
b_{2}(z_{1},z_{2})
as pointing upwards in Figure
4.8
. Similarly, the flow from
\tilde{\gamma}_{2}^{\prime\prime}(z_{1},z_{2})
approaches
g(q_{2},q_{1}^{\prime\prime})
from the left. Hence a segment of
I_{3}
consists of points of
rl
type, which implies the claim.
∎

Finally we claim that each horizontal line below
z_{2}=z_{2}(P_{1})
intersects
S_{1}
once and each vertical line to the left of
z_{1}=z_{1}(P_{1})
intersects
S_{2}
once inside
U_{\tilde{q}}
, as shown in Figure
4.5
. This can be shown by an argument similar to our analysis of MFTS corresponding to
(z_{1}^{\prime},0)
and we omit it here. Hence
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are as shown in Figure
4.5
and intersect at a unique point. This point corresponds to an MFTS with
1
switch converging to
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
. By a similar analysis it is the only MFTS with
1
switch starting at
(x_{2},x_{1})
and hence
\mu^{2}_{M}(x_{1},x_{2})=\bm{\gamma}+\hbar\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
.

Figure 4.8
.
The pair
(\tilde{\gamma}_{1}^{\prime},\tilde{\gamma}_{2}^{\prime})
at switching moments on the MFTS corresponding to
(z_{1}^{\prime},0)
(on the left) and to
(0,z_{2}^{\prime})
(on the right). The gray dots are points of tangency.

4.3.
The Katok examples and the vanishing of the higher
A_{\infty}
-operations

Continuing with the proof of Theorem
1.7
, in order to show the vanishing of the higher
A_{\infty}
-operations, we now switch from the standard metric on
S^{2}
to a certain family
F_{\lambda}
,
\lambda\in[0,1)
, of Finsler metrics on
S^{2}
, often called the “Katok examples” (see
[
Kat73
]
). The explicit formula for
F_{\lambda}
,
\lambda\in[0,1)
, is given by

(4.11)

F_{\lambda}=\frac{\sqrt{\left(1-\lambda^{2}\sin^{2}r\right)dr^{2}+\sin^{2}rd\phi^{2}}-\lambda\sin^{2}rd\phi}{1-\lambda^{2}\sin^{2}r},

in the standard polar geodesic coordinates on
S^{2}
with
(r,\phi)\in(0,\pi)\times[0,2\pi]
. Let
p=(\tfrac{\pi}{2},0)
and
q_{\varepsilon}=(\tfrac{\pi}{2},2\pi\varepsilon)
, with
\varepsilon>0
small, be points on the equator
r=\tfrac{\pi}{2}
.

The following
approximate monotonicity
property holds, which in turn will enable us to show that
\mu^{d}=0
for
d\geq 3
.

Lemma 4.10

(Approximate monotonicity)
.

Given a sequence
N_{i}\to\infty
, there exist sequences
\lambda_{i}\to 1
with
\lambda_{i}
irrational, and
\varepsilon_{i}\to 0
such that:

(1)

for all
F_{\lambda_{i}}
-geodesics
\gamma
from
p
to
q_{\varepsilon_{i}}
of Morse index
\leq N_{i}
, the Morse index
\operatorname{ind}(\gamma)
is approximately proportional to the
F_{\lambda_{i}}
-length
\mathscr{L}_{F_{\lambda}}(\gamma)
, i.e., there exists
C_{i}
close to
\pi
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
small such that
|C_{i}\cdot\operatorname{ind}(\gamma)-\mathscr{L}_{F_{\lambda}}(\gamma)|<\varepsilon^{\prime}
; and

(2)

all other
F_{\lambda_{i}}
-geodesics have
F_{\lambda_{i}}
-length
>C_{i}N_{i}
.

Proof.

Let
\gamma_{-}
and
\gamma_{+}
be the two simple
F_{\lambda}
-geodesics from
p
to
q_{\varepsilon}
along the equator and
\gamma_{\pm}^{m}
be the
F_{\lambda}
-geodesic from
p
to
q_{\varepsilon}
in the direction of
\gamma_{\pm}
which passes
m
times through the antipodal point of
p
, such that
\gamma_{-}=\gamma_{-}^{0}
and
\gamma_{+}=\gamma_{+}^{1}
.
The
F_{\lambda}
-lengths of the geodesics are given by

(4.12)

\mathscr{L}_{F_{\lambda}}(\gamma_{-}^{m})=\frac{2\pi(m+\varepsilon)}{1+\lambda},\quad\mathscr{L}_{F_{\lambda}}(\gamma_{+}^{m})=\frac{2\pi(m-\varepsilon)}{1-\lambda}.

The Morse index of
\gamma_{\pm}^{m}
is given by the number of points that are conjugate to
p
along
\gamma_{\pm}^{m}
. The
\phi
-angle distance of
\gamma_{-}^{m}
is equal to
2\pi(m+\varepsilon)
and by
[
Zil83
, p.145, l.-11]
the
\phi
-angle distance between two consecutive conjugate points along
\gamma_{-}^{m}
is equal to
\pi(1+\lambda)
. Therefore,

(4.13)

\operatorname{ind}(\gamma_{-}^{m})=\left\lfloor\tfrac{2(m+\varepsilon)}{1+\lambda}\right\rfloor.

Similarly we have:

(4.14)

\operatorname{ind}(\gamma_{+}^{m})=\left\lfloor\tfrac{2(m-\varepsilon)}{1-\lambda}\right\rfloor.

Now
[
Rob07
, Theorem 2 and Proposition 6]
with
W=\partial_{\phi}
and
\sigma=0
(using the notation there), supplies a correspondence between geodesics and conjugate points on geodesics starting at
p
with respect to the round metric and those with respect to
F_{\lambda}
(also see
[
Rob07
, p.1642,l.-3]
for an explicit description of the geodesics). Using this correspondence one can show that the points where geodesics starting at
p
intersect the equator
r=\tfrac{\pi}{2}
are exactly the conjugate points along
\gamma_{\pm}^{m}
, which is equal to:

(4.15)

\operatorname{Conj}_{p}=\left\{(\tfrac{\pi}{2},\varphi^{k}_{\pm})\in S^{2}\mid\varphi^{k}_{\pm}=\pi(1\pm\lambda)k\operatorname{mod}2\pi,\,k=1,2,3,\dots\right\}

Given an irrational
\lambda
we may pick
\varepsilon\ll\lambda
such that
q_{\varepsilon}
is not a conjugate point of
p
and hence all geodesic paths from
p
to
q_{\varepsilon}
are contained in the equator.

For an irrational
\lambda
it is straightforward to show that the sequence of indices of
\gamma_{\pm}^{m}
coincides with the sequence of all nonnegative integers after rearranging (with no repetitions). Given
N\gg 0
, if we take
\lambda>1-\frac{2}{N+1}
, then
\operatorname{ind}(\gamma_{+}^{1})=\lfloor(N+1)(1-\varepsilon)\rfloor>N
for
\varepsilon>0
small by Equation (
4.14
). Hence the
F_{\lambda}
-geodesics of index
\leq N
are
\gamma_{-}^{m}
,
m=1,\dots,N
. (1) and (2) then follow from Equations (
4.12
) and (
4.13
).
∎

Katok examples and Morse theory.

At this point we recall that
L=F_{\lambda}^{2}
is not a smooth Lagrangian function on
TM
and in fact is only
C^{1}
along the zero section. Hence we cannot directly apply Theorem
2.2
. There are various remedies in the literature allowing one to develop Morse theory in the Finsler setting. We adopt the approach of
[
Lu15
]
and
[
GX20
]
and pick a smooth approximation of
L
, i.e., by
[
GX20
, Lemma 5.3]
there exists a family of modifications
L_{\eta}
of
L_{0}=L
parametrized by
\eta\in[0,1]
, such that each
L_{\eta}
is a smooth nonnegative Lagrangian satisfying (L1), (L2) and the following on
TM
:

(a)
L_{0}(x,v)-\eta\leq L_{\eta}(x,v)\leq L_{0}(x,v)
for all
(x,v)\in TM
,

(b)
L_{\eta}(x,v)=L_{0}(x,v)
for
L_{0}(x,v)\geq\eta
.

Claim 4.11
.

For
\eta
sufficiently small, all the critical points of
\mathscr{A}_{L_{\eta}}
coincide with those of
\mathscr{A}_{L}
.

Proof.

Let
\gamma
be a critical point of
\mathscr{A}_{L_{\eta}}
with
\eta
small.
There exists
\eta^{\prime}=\eta^{\prime}(\eta)
such that
\lim_{\eta\to 0}\eta^{\prime}(\eta)=0
and if
L(\gamma(t_{0}),\dot{\gamma}(t_{0}))>\eta^{\prime}
for some
t_{0}\in[0,1]
, then the curve
\gamma
is a Finsler geodesic by (b). On the other hand, if
L(\gamma(t),\dot{\gamma}(t))\leq\eta^{\prime}
for all
t\in[0,1]
, then
\mathscr{A}_{L}(\gamma)\leq\eta^{\prime}<(\frac{2\pi\varepsilon}{1+\lambda})^{2}
, where
(\frac{2\pi\varepsilon}{1+\lambda})^{2}
is the smallest value of
\mathscr{A}_{L}
amongst all the paths from
p
to
q
.
∎

Hence, for
\eta
sufficiently small,
\mathscr{A}_{L_{\eta}}
has only Finsler geodesics of
L
as its critical points and the action
\mathscr{A}_{\sqrt{L_{\eta}}}
of each of these geodesics is given by (
4.12
), while the Morse indices are given by (
4.13
) and (
4.14
). Additionally, Theorem
2.2
